PRACTICAL I POULTRY FARMING.

Pzi3aworzrf

[BY REDC

_ From the " Feathered World."

TIIE LAST CHAPTER. The time has now come when I must bid farewell to my readeis until next season. I look back over the past six months with no small amount of satisfaction. This is the third year that we have had the poultry column. When I have thought of those near home my m.nd is carried away to Canada and America where Redcap's notes are read. It is very pleasing to know that one's efforts have not been in vain. I have many communications (personal and otherwise) which testify that the poultry column as been mutually advantageous. A special feature this season has been the illustrations. About 25 different breeds and varieties have been pictured in these columns. This has only been made possible by the kindness of" The Feathered World, " a publication which I have no hesitation in recommending to all my readers. It is hoped that the illustrations have been helpful. It is very much easier to understand what certain varieties are like by this method than it is by writing. For that purpose the pictures were introduced. Last year I concluded my remarks by giving work in the poultry yard in calendar form for the remaining part of the year. I have been asked by many readers to do 80 again. I readily concur with the idea because I think it a good plan. So we NNill start with the month of

As far as the weather -is—C:3-ncerned at present, we have not much to complain aas regards heat. How- ever, this will undoubtedly come. In the meantime make provision. Provide a nice shady shelter for tue youngsters. This can easily he done with a few broken branches of Sycamore. They are now in full leaf and easily obtainable. THE EARLY PULLET.

Do not force the early pullets too much. Let them develop natni ally. Toe food for these should be a morning mash of biscuit meal and thirds with grain food at night of oats and wheat. The early cockerels should be hurried on. Give three plenty of good fattening food and get them on the market, they vi ill fetch very good prices this month aud next. • AUGUST. A good deal that has been said for July is applicable to this month also. A good many of the stock will now be in moult. During this period they should be kept unusually warm because it helps them to get through the moult much quicker. At the first signs of moult, however, do not feel too cell._ Just starve them a little so as to get the old feather sou the move. When nicely started keep them together in close confinement, when their feathers will shed like leaves. During the moult they should be fed liberally and on the most nourishing food ; add a little sulphur to the soft food. An occasional feed of hempseed is good, as the oil frL in the seed is very beneficial to the fowl's body especially during the moulting period. During this month you may put down a few batches of eggs from the table birds. Thee will come in for Christmas-time. SEMEMRER AND OCTOBER.

Work in the poultry yard during these two autumn months should be of a busy nature. All lepairs among,t the houses bhuild be d.,ne. Every care should now be taken against the coming winter. Take all chicken coops and foster-rnothe s and clean them well before storing away for the winter. Gather aod store away a good supply of leaves. These form very good material fiz the scratching sheds. The early pullets should now be hastened on. Add a little flesh meat to the soft food but avoid giving forcing con- diments. Stock birds that are be;ng kept for another season's work should be given an entire rest. Get them through the moult and then seperate them until the next breeding season comes round. NoVE \USER AND DECEMBER. The sun is now shining through my window, I can see the flowers in full bloom. The birds are chit ping their merry song— and it is most difficult to either think of,or even write about winter. But I suppose it will come along as usual. During these two mouths things in the poultry yard are s,,mewhat dormant. Special attention should be paid to the winter-laying stock, so that they do not get overfat— encourage them to occupy the scratching shed a good deal. Bury a few handful of small seeds amongst the. leaves, once they find them they will scratch on until all are found. This keeps them in good exercise and therefore in good laying condition. Those who keep fowls in close confinement will have need to get in a stock of man- golds as a green food substitute. During the vet), sevele weather give a few feeds of maize but do not overfeed, keep the runs d,y, the bigger the scratch- ing shed the better. Fowls in winter if kept in a good big warm scratching shed will do well, but, on the other hand if they are subjected to the severe weather they will not do well. The productive period in the life of a fowl is during the sururr er months, there- , lore it is essential that you make the v inter condi- ' tion3 as ,much like summer as possible. _ _ . QUERIES.

I have just one question to hand. This came too late to be dealt with last week in this column, and although I have personally visited the questioner I will deal with the matter for the benefit of others who 'may be similarly perplexed ."(A Beginner)" writes as follows :—I bought a hen and 12 day old chicks six weeks ago. The mother hen has now commenced to Jay and it seems to me she wants to leave the chicks. Would another broody hen take charge of the chicks, or are they old enough to be left alone? Answer : Seeing that the weather is warm the chicks are quite old enough to look after themselves. It scmetimes happens however that this sort of' thing occurs before the chickens are much age. methods have to be adopted. I hare known broodies to lay and brood chicks at the same time. Others on the contrary become ' peevish and peck at the chicks. When this happens, something most be done. If you have another hen which has been brocdy for some time, you , might try her with the chicks. But always do so at night. Put the chicks under the hen at dark and watch the hen. If when she hears the chicks she seerns contented and huddles them under her, you can rest assured that she means to brood them.

THE LAST WORD.

In conclusion, I remember thai-i promised to deal further with the common diseases of poultry. I find, however, that my space is exhausted. Therefore, readers, all that I can ask you to do is to write me when in trouble. Although my column closes now for the season, you ate at liberty to send in any question you like to the " Examiner " Office. I shalt only be too pleased to help you. Meanwhila good luck to all my readers, and may you 'end up,' with a right successful season.

BOWLING.

BRITISH CROWN GREEN ASSOCIATION.

LANCASHIRE'S VICTORY OVER DERBYSHIRE Derbyshire, along with Staffordshire, made their first appearance in this year's mtampionship of the British Crown Green Amateur Association, the first-named county opposing Lancushire, and Staffordshire meeting Yorkshire. For the second year the championship (which carries with it the Crosfleld Cup and gold medals) will te decided in a home-and-home system, 24 players from each county-12 away and 12 at borne— taking part in matches which are decided on the aggre- gate total. Lancashire have already met and defeated Yorkshire by the narrow majority of 8 points. Lancashire selected the Pendleton Belmont Green, Salford, for the home match, the Whaley Bridge Green being the venue for Derbyshire home team to oppose Lancashire. Yotk- shire changed their venue to Cltckbeaton, where the Staffordshire team were visitors, the Yorkthire av,ny team opposing Staffordshire on the Death Town Green, Wolverhampton. Results . The Examiner, Saturday, June, 29, 1912.

LESSONS OF THE COAL STRIKE.

SIDELIGHTS ON RELIEF IN WARRINGTON.

"BARE-FACED ATTEMPTS AT DECEIT."

Although the national coal strike is now, happily, only a memory, it has left its lessons which shoulo be of considerable guidance in the administration of relief in any other case of keen and widespread emergency. The Warrington Distiess Committee, which distributed the fund raised locally during the crisis, formally completed its labours on Tuesday even- ing when a meeting was held in the Committee Chamber at the TOM' Hall to receiie the report and accounts. The report of the Investigation Committee, while containing a record of remarkable activity on the part of all concerned in the relief measures, did not make altogether pleasant reading, for the com- mittee felt it their duty to pass severe strictures on the deceits practised by the undeserving, and to call attention to some of the seamy phases of life, as revealed by the sifting of the cases before them. The Mayor (Domini ior Dr. Joseph) presided over the meeting and there vas a fair attendance.

THE COMMITTEE'S STRIGTURES. STARTLING DISCLOSURES,

The report of the Investigation Committee, after detailing the steps taken to adiginister relief, places on record for guidance 00 future occasions 'a few facts and recommendations.' The committee are of opinion that it is undesirable on the first cry of alarm to take emergency measures. Whilst your committee are pleased to record that the majority of those dealt with were very deserving oases

SOM E DESERVING CASES (amongst whom were " spongers") were relieved on the first occasion, and those who are always ready to ontain something for nothing, whether deserving or not, make every effort to take advantage of such panic relie4. Even at the risk of a little preventible suffering, it would be desirable in future to have a thorough investi- gation prior to the distribution of relief. No relief should be given to single men whose responsibilities are few, as it should be more easy for them to make some provision against possible emergencies. The committee were impressed with the fact that a number of those who weie earliest and most insistent in their applica- tions were the

===== 0002601_19120629_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
HIGHER. PAID INIEN

in connection with-Ca";ijne -i-r:crut:r-Ms. The labourers and lower paid emplo3ers were amongst the last to come "orward. This perhaps is due to the tact that many of the latter are continually living on the verge of starva- tion, and are thus more preparea to meet privation and distress. It is with regret that we have to record our opinion that by neglecting to exercise a little thrift in prosperous times many of the better paid workers, Szc., were obliged to have recourse to the public fund in the early stages of distribution. There was very marked evidence during the course of the enquirits that a greater prop.,rtion of the wages earned by many of these men was not spent on the home, and we are left to assume was SPENT ON THEIR PERSONAL PLEASURES ...

to the dlsardvantage and loss of the wives and families. In many cases after the strike was over, applications for relief were made by women, who, when informed that no relief could be given as their husbands were working, stated the amount of money their husbands had given then] the previous weok, and in nearly every case the amount fell very far short of the man's actual receipts as given by bia employer. As a single example, of whi( h there were many instances, a man had given his wife 12s. 6d., she being under the impression that he was only earning ss. a turn, whereas the works reported to the Investigation Committee that in that particular week he would have a clear wage of 28s. Repeatedly the women said that they did not know what their husbands were earning even in normal times. It. appears to the com- mittee that it is a pressing necessity that kteps should be taken whereby the bigger proportion of the earnings of these workpeople should be used fur the support and maintenance of their wives and families. It is not perhaps for the committee to make suggestions, but they put this forward for the consideration of those who are concerned with such problems. , It is almost incredible that men and women, particu- larly women, would dare to venture through the streets to the relief office in tbe

FILTHY CONDITION in which many of them were. Several of the women were not only dirty but also verminous. So unpleasant was the smell brought into the office by these people that the distributors were allowed to smoke, and even then it required the doors and windows to be kept well open to prevent the staff engaged from being overcome by the odours. The Committee feel thst the state in which these women and also their children are to be found may be attributed to the lack of means and the want of encouragement by the husband,who takes little interest or pride in his children or his home. It is not bard to undersund how women with families of five, six, or more childrem, receiving from their husbands anything up to, but not above 20s. or 255. a week, out of which they have to provide plenty of food (and even clothing in some cases) for the husband, besides paying the rent: of the house, gradually feel thst they are losing ground and as time goes on they are more and more satisfied to drift, with consequent depression and carelessness of such details as cleanliness, while the earlier habits learned at school, in their mother's home, or in service are quickly forgotten. To produce some improvement in the condition in which these men and women must be passing their lives it would probably be of advantage if, on their being summoned to any public office, such as the Guardians, the Health Office, the Education Office, or the Police Court, they should be spoken to in such a manner as will shame them, for during the strlke it was found that by calling attention to their filthy state consider- able improvement was brought about, and during the last week there was much less cause for complaint, Another matter which is a cause for surprise and regret is the

13ARF.-FACED ATTEMPTS AT DECEIT. In a large number of crumb it was only after very care- ful cross-examination of the applicants and enquiry from the works that it was possible to get at the truth. In fact at one works it was stated that a man had been employed during the strike on white-washing, for which he was paid, and when he came before the works committee for his recommend for relief he kept this matter from the manager of his works, and in another case something like 30a. out-of-work pay was being received by the family, and yet they had the audacity to make application for relief. These csass are mentioned as showing the necessity, not only in time of diffaculty,such as the recent coal strike, but in normal times, of the closest investigation being made before relief is granted in any form. A lack of discretion was displayed in making purchases on the first day's issue of tickets. Many small purchases including delicsces were made, but as soon as this came to the committee's notice, the tradesmen were instructed to supply only what was indicated on the form.

In concluding this report we desire to place on record our indebtedness to all who have in any way assisted our investigations or distributions, and without wish- ing to overlook or to make invidious distinctions feel that the following should be specially mentioned:- 1 Subscribers to the Mayor's Fund in money and in kind. 2 Voluntary Organisers and helpers at the works and at tbe Bewsey Adult School Soup Kitchens. 3 Employers and Employees for their contribu- tions to the Soup Kitchens. 4 Those who gave in kind to the Soup Kitchens. Messrs Richmond and Co. for the loan of boilers. 5 Those Employers who kept their workpeople employed at considerable extra cost during the strike. 6.—The Clergy and Ministers, Works Managers and Foremen at Messrs. Pearson and Knowles, Monks Hall, and Whitecross Co., members of the Guild of Help, Poor Law Officers and Lady Inspec- tors of the Health Department for verification and recommendation of cases for assistance, the local Press and their representatives, the Chief Constable, officers and men of the Borough Police Force, the Director of Education and staff of his department, the Borough Treasurer and staff of his department, and the Town Clerk and staff of his department. The balance which is left over (£3Bl ha), out of which sundry grants have yet to be paid), may appear large, but the reason for this is explained by the following facts : I Your Committee towards the termination of the relief efforts approached the works managers and obtained their valuable opinion its to the continuation of your Committee's work. The opinion was unanimously expressed that owing to the rapid resump- tion of work there was no necessity to prolong relief, but your Committee in many cases where it might be desirable had intended to continue for a longer period to grant assistance or relief in reclaiming pledges, and also in giving the workers some help in paying off "subs" which had been obtained from employers, and thus enable the worker in a shorter period to feel the benefit of his full earnings, but it is not now thought that this step is necessary or desirable. Your Committee recommend that the balance of the fund be handed over to the Mayor rto be invested in Corporation Bonds in the name of the Mayor for the time being, and to be held for the purpose of alleviat- ing future distress in the Borough. The report is signed by the Mayor, Councillor H Roberts, Mr J M Murray (Director of Education), Mr A Bottomley (clerk to the Guardians),Mr W J Rowland (secretary of the Guild of Help), and the Town Clerk, who are members of the Investigation Committee. ' On the motion of the MAvolt,the report was adopted. THE DISPOSAL OF TIIE BALANCE. £2OO TO BE INVESTED.

It was decided that the following recommendations of the Investigation Committee for dealing with the funds in their bands be adopted : " That the Executive Committee be recommended to

S:c, £5 ; school children's meals, Education Committee (balance), £33 75." ----- Mr HOPE pointed out that the repayments were stated to be to the various firms, but in nearly every case the relief had been disp,msed from special funds belonging to the workmen. The repayments would, therefore, be to the workmen's organizations. The Rev H OGDEN : IS the money a repayment of the amount spent by the men's organizations ? Mr HOPE : A partial repayment—a very small proportion. The MAYOR : It is paid for the relief of those who were outside the works organizations.

===== 0002601_19120629_art0028.txt | included=true | included =====
VOTES OF THANKS.

Councillor BELLIAN moved :—" That the best thanks of this meeting be given to the Executive Committee, members of the Investigation Committee, and all who have so ably and satisfactorily assisted in raising and distributing funds for the relief of distress during the coal strike." The constitution of the committee had, he said, been fully justified by their excellent work and the straightforward report they had presented. Father TALLON seconded, and the motion was carried. Mr H HOPE moved a vote of thanks to Mr 3 M Murray and Mr T H Spinks for their valuable services. (Applause.) He said they had worked long hours in connection with the relief fund, in fact. he had never known any two gentlemen put in better work for a charitable object. (Applause). The MAYOR seconded, and said that from his own , personal observation he could fully corroborate what Mr Hope had said. Mr Murray and Mr Spinks had not an enviable task, and their work had been invaluable. Mrs. SIMPSON, in supporting, acknowledged the advice which the officials named were always willing to give to the workers at the liewsey Adult School. The vote was heartily agreed to. Mr. MURRAY, in returning thanks, said it was not the first time he had been associated with the carrying , through of a distress fund, but it was an experience he would never forget. He thought before the strike took place, that he knew something about Warrington people but he knew nothing compared with what he learned during the strike. Any success that had attended the distribution of relief was due entirely to the hearty co-opqration of the other officials of the Corporation, who assisted in it but, what was more important still, to the efforts of those who stayed in the office, and put in s I much extra work. (Hear, hear). Mr. SPINES said his part had been a small one. The success obtained was due to the hearty co-operation they got from all sources, particularly from the works Without that help their own work would not have counted for much. .

Mr HOPE also moved the following : "That the hearty thanks of this meeting be given to his Worship the Mayor for (1) opening a mayoral fund, (2) for his efforts in alleviating distress during the strike, (3) for acting as chairman and (4) for the use of the Town Hal!. (Applause).

===== 0002601_19120629_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
THE M 'YOR AND STRIKES.

- Mr Myr/RAY seconded, and said no appeal had ever been made to the Mayor which had not been readily responded to. This motion was helrtily carried. The MAYOR, in acknowledging the vote, said he did his best under very distressful circumstances. He took a great deal of interest in seeing what could be done for the poor people. Occupying the position of mayor one could not help feeling an enormous amount of responsibility. The office was by no means an ornamental one at any time, but in a crisis like that occasioned by the coal strike one would have felt glad to be relieved of the position. At the same time it was something to be able to feel that one had satisfied one's conscience and done the right thing. The people of Warrington behavediadmirably during the strike, and the poor had a great deal to be thankful for in respect to the manner in which they were treated by their brethren who were more fortunate than themselves. Without egotism, it could be claimed that the people in Warrington:who were well off seemed to be more kindly disposed towards their poorer townsmen than well-to-do people in some other districts. He hoped that what took place during the strike would lead to a much better feeling between rich and poor and have the effect of preventing strikes and other occurrences due to misunderstandings. (Hear, hear, and applause.) On the nroposition of Mr A BOTTOMLEY, seconded by the Rev II OGDEN, a vote of thanks was accorded to the Town Clerk and the Borough Treasurer for their work on behalf of the fund.

For the encouragement of mixed bathing, one of the proprietors of swimming baths at Fllackpool bas made a regulation that on Wednesday afternoons no gentle- man is to be admitted to the baths unless accompanied by a lady. A charge against Mrs Caroline Garner, of The Beeches, Liverpool-road, Cadishead, of being drunk in charge of a horse and trap in Station-road, Urmston, on the 20th, was dismissed on Tuesday at the Manchester County Police Court.

===== 0002601_19120629_art0064.txt | included=true | included =====
The Examiner, Saturday, June, 29, 1912.

===== 0002601_19120629_art0065.txt | included=true | included =====
deliberation in allocating the surplus. The simpler and fairer explanation, that the Chancellor's motives were just what he stated, has been completely justified. As soon as the contingencies had translated themselves into facts, the Government policy in regard to the surplus was made public, and it is a policy with which the Opposition could hardly find any fault.

One million of the THE NATION'S surplus has been BALANCE. SHEET appropriated to the Navy. Half-a-million is to be employed as a loan for the extension and development of our resources in East Africa and Uganda. The stimulus this will administer to the opening up of a new area of cotton-growing will be of material service to the textile industries of Lancashire. There remain five millions, the whole of which will be applied to the extinction of dept. When the opposition attack the Government for the growth of expenditure—an expenditure which they themselves have often attempted to increase and against the main heads of which they can raise no objection—they will only render their own position ridiculous if they do not take into account the Govern- ment's exceptional record in the reduction of national liabilities. It would be a queer kind of business criticism which attacked the management of a firm whose prosperity was growing every year, on the ground that its gross expenditure, mainly on reproductive items, was also growing, while its liabilities were steadily being reduced.

The astonishing record A RECORD IN of the present Govern- NATIONAL SAVING ment in the liquidation of the nation's liabilities can best be set out in tabular form, showing what different Chancellors of the Exchequer have done': Period hi Years. Debt Reduction Ooschen ... £38,500.000

The members of the I OUR " SUBMERgrED Distress Committe e TENTH." which operated during the period of the coal strike in Warrington, seem to have made many discoveries as a result of their closer acquaintance with the "submerged tenth's of Warrington's population. A report issued by the Investigation Committee (and printed in another column) is an illuminating, if not very cheering, document. Strike privations brought the usually hidden dwellers of the slums into the light of day, , and their capacity for carrying dirt and ' indulging in deceit has shocked the relief officers and workers. " it is almost increditable," the repert says, that men and women particularly women, wf uld date to venture through the streets in the filthy condition in which many of them were." As a rule these unfortunate folks are safely hidden away in loathsome hovelswhich have escaped the attention of the Health Com- mittee. Obviously such people are more to be pitied than blamed, and the remedy lies in a more aggressive sanitary administration. , Another factor which contributed to the enlightenment of the distress committee was the conduct of so many husbands who, on their wives `showing,earned a good wage and spent a large proportion on" their personal pleasures "—the public house understood, in most cases. Sometimes the wife was in ignorance of what her husband really earned —are husbands decievers ever ?—which only aggravates the offence. Then too, The Committee were impressed with the fact that a number of those who were earliest and mist insistent in their application were the higher paid men." In their case a common want of thrift and provision for the rainy day" brought famine early to the door. The chronically poor seem to have been better able to meet privation for the unpleasant reason that they are more or less inured to it. Altogether the distress has let a flood of light into some very dark places. The social "sores" are more apparent than ever, and the task of the reformer looms gigantic before him. Only one hopeful gleam is discernible through the cloud, and that emanates from the full and clear understanding of the position which those in authority must have obtained. A want of such understanding has long stood in the way of the Health Committee, which we shall hope to see pressing forward in the future with the full mandate of civic authority.

Preparations for the Old English Fair to be held in Victoria Park on July 11th, 12th and 13th, in aid of the Infirmary Million Penny Fund, are practically complete. The committee announce a unique attrac- tion, in addition to the host of amusements charcteristic of the fair ground. By arrangement with the proprietors of the" Daily Mail," Mr W H Ewen, the renowned aviator, known as "The Flying Scot," will alight in Victoria Park about 5 p.m. on Friday, the 12thguly,and be received by his Worship the Mayor (Dr G WJoseph).His machine, a 60-h.p. Caudron Bi-plane will be on view in an enclosure on the ground at an extra chlrge, Picture poitcards of the aviator will be on sale at Id. each, and it will be possible to send these by aerial post at an extra charge of ld. to cover Government stamp and expenses, if deposited in the special post office on the ground. Provision will be made for advance posting on Thursday, July 11th. We are asked to state that all dolls intended for the doll competition must be delivered at the Infirmary not later than Saturday next.

WOMAN'S BREAKING POINT.

Many women believe that the pains which they are called upon to bear are unavoidable. "I believed that what 1 suffered was Nature's law," writes a woman of North London.. " You have shown me that I need not have suffered at all. I realise now that a woman can be spared this useless pain." Thousands of women do not receive, or fail to believe the message of relief. They go on silently, uncomplainingly— almost recklessly--suffering, until at last they can go on no longer. The time comes when the breaking point is reached, and they are fortunate if with physical wreck there does not come mental wreck also. Deeply conscious of these things and of women's aversion to speaking of them, Mrs. Lydia E. Pinkham, a gracious and talented lady, set herself the task of making known a medicine which she knew would enable women to fortify themselves against their ills without calling in outside aid. Success crowned her efforts, and Lydia E. Pinkham's Vegetable Compound is known to-day to women of all civilised countries. Mrs. Lyaia E. Pinkham herself had to give up trying to cope single-handed with the world-wide demand which sprang up for her Medicine, and now it may be obtained from any chemist for 2s. 9d. a bottle, or 4a. 6d. for a very large bottle. All women should write for a free book sent in a plain envelope by the Lady Manager, c/o Lydia E. Pinkham Medicine Co., 42a, Hampstead Road, London, N.W. She will also send Lydia E. Pinkham's Vegetable Compound direct, post free, at the same price.

liletlm Of Delusion.

Mr. Brighouse held an inquiry at the Police Station on Thursday, into the circumstances of the death of Thomas William Peers, aged 35 years, a shoe pressnaqn of 84, Winwick-Street, Warrington.—The widow said her husband told her there was a gang of men who had got down the cellar grid and make their way into his bedroom by five ways, and that the best thing he could do was to give himself up to the police. He had made Iwo attempts to strangle himself the same week. He had gambled away a legacy, and that had preyed on his mind.—P.C. Leonard said deceased called at the police station in the evening and said his wife had just taken his two children to bed and turned him out, and he bad nowhere to go. The body was recovered from the river Mersey on Tuesday.— A verdict of " Suicide while of unsound mind" was returned.

Lads' Fatal Fall An inquest was held at the Warrington Coroner's Court on Monday by Mr. F. A. Jones (Deputy Coroner) on the body of Edward Hart, aged eight years, of 6, Melville-street. The evidence showed that two months ago the lad fell on the kerbstone and hurt his head, but however a short time later he appeared to have recovered from the fall. On Thursday be corn- Flamed of a pain in his stomach, but after a time appeared to be all right. The following day, as he did not seem well, his grandmother obtained a "recommend" from the Infirmary, and he was seen by Dr. Manson. During Friday night he began to vomit and died on Saturday morning.—Dr. Manson said when he attended the deceased he was suffering from an open supperating wound in the forehead and seemed very ill. He considered the question of send- ing him to the Infirmary, but be seemed to be too ill to move. He attributed death to inflammation of the brain, which was a result of the injury, A verdict of "Accidental death" was returned. TENNIS, Croquet, Cricket and other outdoor gnmer.— J. S. GARLAND, 51-55, Bridge St., Warrington.—Advt. The Territorials.

The 4th Battalion Prince of Wales' Volunteers (South Lancashire Regiment) under the command of Lieutenant Colonel B. Fairclough, paraded on Satur- day on the lawn in front of the Town Hall at 3 p.m. After inspection of Companies, they marched off via Sankey-street, Bridge-street, and Stockton Heath, headed by the fife and drums and the Battalion Band, to Appleton Park, which was kindly lent for the occasion by Mr T H Lyon. On arrival each Company was exercised at drill Sic by its own Company officers, after which refreshments were issued. The Battalion, on again falling in, were sized by the Companies, and ceremonial drill was carried out. At the conclusion, points were placed, and the March Past was practised, both in column and quarter column. The Battalion returned to headquarters, leaving Appleton Park at 7.30 pm. and were formed up in front of the Town Hall, where Territorial Efficiency Medals ware presented by Lieut.-Colonel B Fairclough to the undernamed members of the Battalion Colour Sergt W Roberts H Company, Private G Heath A Company, Private E Melling A Company. The Battalion was then dismissed, the Newton-le-Willows Detachment (E and G) returning to their own head- quartero under the command of Captain P. N. Avery. The numbers present on parade were 310 all ranks, including the following officers :—Lieut.-Colonel B Fairclough (in command), Major F M Appleton V.D. (2nd in command), Capt G Elgood (adjutant). Capts. Ft Smith, 11. G Roberts, P N Avery ; Lieuts. Egerton Yairclough,"M Woods, W McClure, L S Henshall, Second-Liants C Collingwood. C H Robertson and Lieut and Quarter-gaster GH Ingram. The strength of the Battalion in now 010 al ranks ; 02 are still required to complete the full establishment of the Battalion. Young men wishing to join may do soon' application to the Sergeant Major any evening during the week. This year the Battalion will camp at Kirkham, close to Blackpool, from 4th to 18th August. Married Privates attending camp for 15 days will receive allowances for their families. BABY CARRYAGBS, special value at 395. Gd. Go-carts from 10s. GASLAND, 51-55, Bridge at., Warrington.—Advt.

WALKING DAY.

YESTERDAY'S PICTURESQUE

PAGEANTS.

There were many anxious hearts in Warrington yesterday, when heavy rain heralded the dawn of another Aralking Day. The children have, however, been so remarkably favoured in past years—it would be well-nigh impossNe to recall a school festival completely spoiled by bad weather—that few were inclined to believe that the worst would happen on this occasion. At the same time the outlook was sufficiently discouraging to place a severe strain on optimism, and when parents saw their children depart in light summer finery, they were fearful lest in an hour or two they might return looking woe begone and bedraggled. But good fortune ronce more smiled on the day of days and before the processionists had gathered on the lawn in front of the Town Hall, the sun had broken through to give the finishing touch to a scene of brightness and beauty. True, the clouds occasionally lowered threateningly but there was no lain whatever while the scholars were traversing the streets, and the pageant of happy, light-hearted children in pretty dresses lacked none of its wonted attractiveness. It cannot be said that the Walking' Day of 1912 presented much that was new, in fact that would be almost impossible from the very nature of the case. The events of the festival have followed I the same lines with such general acceptance for so many years, that any departure would seem out of place. To give the children a thoroughly happy day is the one consideration of all concerned, and this end is admirably attained by the combination of pageantry and festivity which invests the anniversary with glamour for the The schools made a brave show as they massed on the greensward in front of the Town Hall. By 930 children were streaming up the drive and the lawn was soon a blaze of colour and animation. • The blare of brass filled the air, and the music was as varied as the number and composition of the bands would lead one to expect. Sprightly, popular ditties, sober martial airs, and solid hymn tunes were all discoursed with inharmonious effect to those who were in a position to hear many bands at once. From the Town Hall steps a number of prominent townspeople watched the great muster. They included the Mayor and Mayoress (Councillor Dr. Joseph and Mrs. A. Nield), Mr. and Mrs. A. H. Crosfield, Miss Crosfield, Alderman and Mrs. J. Smethurst. The marshalling of the procession was undertaken by Mr. H. T. Fair- dough and Mr. Lionel Fairclough. As usual, the police, under the direction of the Chief Constable (Mr. M. Nicholls), bad made excellent arrangements to ensure the processionists a clear passage through the streets and there was no overlapping, and, con- sequently, few irritating stoppages. Promptly, at ten o'clock, the Blue Coat school led the way through the centre gate of the Park, and the procession passed out in the following order : (1) Blue Coat School and Band , (2) Padgate Cottage Homes and Band (3) Fairfield School and Crosfield's Band (4) National School (5) St. George's Mission and Lymm Band (6) St. Clement's Mission and Newtoa Farm School Band (7) St. Peter's and Cheshire Lines Band (8) St. James' and Territorial Band (9) St. Ann's and Walker's Brewery Band (10) St. Paul's and Ardwick Green Industrial School Band (11) All Saints' Mission (12) Holy Trinity and Walton Band (13) Factory Yard Mission (14) St. Luke's (15) St. Barnabas' and Preston Brook Band

(16) Emmanuel (17) Buckley-street and Culcheth Cottage Homes Band (18) Salvation Army and Band (19) Ragged School and Band The magnitude of the Procession is best gauged by the fact that it took just an hour to lea7e the Park. It, is adequately described as, in most respects, a replica of its predecessors, but here and there some exceptionally picturesque and novel features arrested the attention. Besides, it was cheering enough to see thousands of radiant youngsters, rejoicing to be, for one day in the year, exalted to proud importance. The Church Lads' Brigade, looking spick and span and marching smartly, was well to the fore. The St. Paul's company paraded 63 strong, there were also GO repre- sentatives of the St. Elphin's, Stockton Heath, Padgate and St. Clement's companies. St. Clement's Mission had a very effective display, Britannia, accompanied by loyal subjects from England, Scotland, Ireland and Wales, walking under a canopy beautifully embellished with roses. St. Peter's school has for some years earned commendation for the beauty of its pageantry, but yesterday it excelled itself. A group of girls were daintily attired in the early Victorian style and comported themselves with quaint dignity. There was also a pretty representati an of " Summer, ' the girls who took part baying their dresses ornamented with pink and violet artificial flowers and very life-like butterflies. They also carried baskets of choice blooms and wands surmounted with flowers. The company of Boy Scouts attached to this school marched with military precision. 'A touch of pleasing simplicity was given to St. Paul's procession by numerous little children carrying baskets of flowers and streamers of ribbon. The workers of All Saints' Mission had put forth special efforts, and admiration was aroused by numerous characters in fancy costumes They included a stately Britannia and a bevy of merry Japanese maids. Floral crowns and garlands were conspicuous in the pretty display of St. Luke's school. Buckley-street Mission maintained its reputation for taste and enterprise, and the spectators were cordial in their appreciation. Little Morris dancers tripped gaily along to the sound of jingling bells and the "old English " flavour of the scene was heightened by the introduction of several maypoles. A picture of con- tentment was a youngster ensconsced in a most artisti- cally decorated mail cart. As usual, the mission made a collection along the route in aid of the Children of Warrington cot in the Infirmary, and point was given to the appeal by the reproduction on a lorry of a scene in a hospital ward. In sharp con- trast to the hosts of gaily dressed, well-cared for children who made up the generality of schools, were the poorest of the poor whom the good-hearted people of the Salvation Army and the Ragged School, make it their pleasure t, entertain on Walking Day. Although many had come from dark homes, they too were obviously realizing something of the joy of the children's day, and too much praise cannot be given to those who bring this ray of happiness into drab and dreary lives.

THE ROMAN CATHOLIC PAGEANT. For exquisite pageantry, everyone would be pre- pared to concede the palm to the Roman Catholics. That love of the ornate and symbolical hich forms a distinct part of their Church sj7stem, prompts them to expend an infinitude of painstaking preparation on processions Of surpassing beauty, and yesterday's display was in keeping with their traditions. The scholars did not assemble on the lawn, but fell in with the general procession in Sankey-street, and the crowds which lined the route were therefore able to , see the whole of the schools' without changing

their positions. St. Mary's school had pride of place and it was succeeded by St. Benedict's, St. Alban's, Sacred Heart, and Our Lady's, Latchford. What made the picture so rich in harmonious colours was that each school had its own distinctive scheme, with the result that there were both diversity and uniformity. Most of the girls had wreaths of flowers on their hair and carried lovely bunches of blooms. A contingent of the St. Benedict's scholars bore silver crooks, tipped with sheaves of corn, and their dresses were studded with poppies. Some of the boys were in smart white suits with red facings. Cheery Irish lads and colleens figured in Our Lady's procession. while a notable feature of the Sacred Heart display was the array of floral devices. Gorgeous banners, depicting the Sacramental ordin- ances and the various patron Saints, were held aloft, and here and there a glittering processional cross with attendant acolytes, arrested the eye. One of the most impressive effects was presented by St. Mary's school, a beautiful white statue of the Blessed Virgin Mary, embowered in flowers, being carried by girls, while other scholars held the long white streamers suspended from the canopy. 'There were several other florally enthroned statues and the procession was notable for the licit masses of natural flowers which were brought into use.

HOW THE DAY WAS SPENT.

In the afternoon the children betook themselves to fields in the neighbourhood where they romped to their hearts' content. The conditions were, for the greater part of the day, propitious and the various programmes of sports were successfully carried out. lollowing their usual practice, most of the Non- conformist chools did not join in the procession round the town but marched direct to the railway station and entrained for rural resorts where fields had been placed at their disposal. Bold-street Wesleyan and Cairo-street Unitarians went to Lymm, Silver-street Wealeyans to Helsby, Wycliffe Congrega- tionalists to Dunham Hill, Workingmen's Mission to Acton Grange, and Legh-stieet Primitive Methodist to Dunham Massey. The usual returns from the schools, giving the numbers of scholars and officers, and particulars of how the day was spent, will be found on page 6.

ROYAL COURT THEATRE.

16 The Bondman."

Hall Caine's popular play "The Bondman "has been a success whenever presented in Warrington, arid the novelty of seeing the drama in the curtailed form necessitated by the two houses a night system has attracted good audiences to the Royal Court Theatre this week. The condensation has been well done, the most enthralling features of the story having been preserved, and there is not an uninteresting moment. The sulphur mines scene, with its startling explosion, is quite a triumph of stagecraft and the mounting of the play throughout is thoroughly adequate. Con- gratulations are due to the management upon securing an exceptionally strong company whose presentment of "The Bondman " is quite the most powerful yet placed on the Royal Court stage. Mr. Arthur Sagor achieves a signal success as Jason. Voice and bear- ing eminently fit him for the part and he makes it live, the intense emotion with which he invests his acting creating a great impression. Mr. F. Paige Lawrence is well placed in the strongly contrasting role of Michael Sun- locks and plays it with alternating buoyancy and sympathy, the scenes in which he is associated with Jason being the most touching. Miss Rita Tomkins is a charming Greeba and rises to the height of her opportunity at the climax of Act 3, when, having delivered Jason into the hands of the authorities as a "spy," she has the mortification of seeing her husband also arrested, Mr. Thomas Barry deserves Epecial mention for his fine study o Father Ferrati, the dissolute priest who is reformed by kindness and trustfulness. Miss Beatrice Sinclair is excellent as the acidulous, sharp-tongued Mrs. Fare- brother and all the other parts are in capable bands. Mr Tom Boothroyd and 'his orchestra play the incidental music very pleasingly. NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME. Next week Miss Ethel Van Praagh and Mr. Edward Whitty's c impany will present the following attractive repertoire :—Monday and Tuesday, 'The eyes of the world " ; Wednesday, " Lady of Lyons " ; Thursday, "David Garrick " ; Friday and Saturday, "Facing the world alone."

PALACE AND HIPPODROME.

We have had so many bright programmes at the Hippodrome within recent weeks that one is apt to wonder if the supply of variety stars is limitless. The management of this popular house is indeed deserving of every praise:for the magnificent way in which they cater for the variety-loving public of Warrington. There is another excellent programme this week, and as usual, the spacious building is being filled nightly. With ease and grace and marvellous dexterity The Beautiful Lilian performs wonderful feats on the bicycle. During the time she occupies the stage the audience are spellbound with her feline grace and daring, and when with a "there you are" smile she dismounts and leaves the stage, she is greeted by tumultuous applause. Her's is undoubtedly one of the most acceptable cycle turns we have seen for some time, and no doubt all would welcame a re-appearance of this charming performer. Of herculean build and fine proportion, the famous Babusio Bros. greatly please with their head to head balancing feats and although there is something of I the circus air about the* turn, it has features which I lift it far above the ordinary acrobatic show, and tue audience are not stinted in their approhatim. Burlesque indeed is the menagerie sketch presented by Charles Baron which abounds In comical "exposures." At the first the audience behold three, apparently fine specimens of the licn family, but as soon as the "tamer's " back is turned one of the " lions " loses its "head " and all the audience can see it is a particularly fine dog." got up." Other "implistures" are on similar tines. A. special feature of the show is the keen race between genuine members of the feline tribe. The whole performance abounds in humour, and the audience is not slow in recognising how much time must have been spent in training the animals to such a pitch of perfection.. Colley and Scott give us a very acceptable entertainment. New and smart gags are introduced to advantage suds capital turn is finished with the ever popular " coon " duet and piano d ah e. a aG w l a e possesses dy s Som a a s good eclever i voice is heard which to advantage in the song ." All that I ask is love." Young and Evans provide some good Hebrew humour and captivate at once with their. breezy style. Tom Parker is indeed a" happy comedian," and , scores heavily with two catchy chorus songs. As a wooden shoe dancer he is also a success. Phil Bransby gives some coster songs- and studies, and although he exaggerates the impersonations at times he is not without talent

.

THE COMEDY---" SMOKE PREVENTION."

As Played by the Warrington Health Committee.

TO THE EDITOR "EXAM IXER." Sir,—As an exhibition of official incapacity extend- ing over the last ten years, commend your readers to the action, or rather inaction, of the Warrington Health Committee on the smoke nuisance. It is fair to say, though strange it may appear, that individually the members of this Comreittee are worthy citizens, who have done and are doing good work for the Borough. It would be difficult to convince these city fathers that they are acting a comedy in their treat- ment of the smoke question. Everyone recognizes it to be a farce except the members themselves, who take themselves and their efforts most seriously. They begin the farce with a series of observations " and in the course of a whole year, 313 working days, actually manage to observe ten cases in which "black smoke was emitted for a longer period than is allowed by the standald fixed by the Committee." Having thus with many " observations " discovered ten cases, they solemnly proceed to deal. v.ith same. A timid, polite letter of remonstrance is written to the offend- lug inanufacturer. The firm in question, recognizing the powers the public have, reply equally politely but without any timidity, that they regret the offence and will try to mend their ways. Pernaps they throw the blame on the fireman and send that individual to be interviewed by the Committee. The Chairman and other members talk very seriously to this useful sub- ordinate who promises to do his best, but probably throws the blame on to the efficiency of his boilers, tells the Committee that he must keep up the steam, and to do it with such inadequate boiler capacity he must fire hard, but promises to do his best to oblige the authorities. With this the Committee are satisfied. A. pleasant feeling pervades the bosoms of those who stand at watch and ward over the public health. They feel they have discharged a painful duty and it is with relief they turn their attention to covering up with lime wash and to clearing away with besoms the dirt the smoke has made. Here they are in their element ; here they are brave as /ions, advanced sanitary reformers, hole they have only the small property owner or householder to deal with, no big firm, no important citizen to get cross with. In a month or two the same farce is again gone through. They have further reports of the same chimneys, polite letters are again exchanged, equally timid on the one side, equally evasive and full of promises of amendment on the other. The letters are received in Committee, read and filed. The Com- mittee having again done enough to satisfy the sense of public duty, again turn to their whitewash and besoms. Meanwhile the offending manufacturers, those indifferent to anything but making money, have their laugh, and dismiss the subject. They know the Warrington Health Committee, and have bad years of experience of their methods. They have friends upon the Committee whom they can trust to see they are not inconvenienced, the matter is forgotten and they turn their attention to the making of dividends out of the making of wire, leather, or ale. -

Why not take the farce off the boards? It has bad a run for over ten years. has ceased to amuse, and the audience is weary ? A growing sense of social obligation. a growing desire for cleaner and 1-"ighter towns and homes, a growing public appreciation of the responsibilities attaching to wealth, an increasing knowledge of the fact that smoke is waste fuel—all demand of the Warrington Health Committee a radical change in their methods. Have we the men on the Health Com- mittee, or on the Council, who can rise to their oppor- tunity and their duty? Q. SMOKE OBSERVATIONS? I WHAT OTHER TOWNS ARE DOING.

The following statistics show how the black smoke ' nuisance is dealt with in other towns. Liverpool seems to make a serious effort to keep the nuisance within bounds and to bring the offenders to book. But in many towns, as in Warrington, the powers of compulsion are practically a dead letter. Warring- ton's ten warnings, and no summonses, for a twelve months' period, do look absurdly inadequate. Can it be doubted that the Health Committee toys with the question?

WHY NOT BE KIPPERED?

. [To lIIE EDITOR GF TIIE " EXAMINER:I Sir,—The Mayor's contention that smoke is a disinfectant is amply borne out not only by the statistics he quotes from the Medical Officer of Health's report, but by the uses that smoke is put to in the preservation of food. The healthiness of a kipper or Aberdeen haddock is due largely to smoke, and why should not the same apply to humanity? A well-kippered population would solve many of the difficulties of the Insurance Act, and so let there be smoke and plenty of it.—Yours ANTI-FAD.

all is the Gambling Inferno, no fanciful picture of poetic imagination, but a real live going concern —a practical and highly profitable "Hell." Could there be a more striking testimony to all the pervading glamour of the game of" gowf "than the fact that insist- ent hoteliers would have it that whatever else Monte Carlo provided it would never be complete without a links of its own. And ceitain it is that no visit to Monte Cello is complete without a sight of those mountain links, with their sporting holes, fine air, and superb views. Equally certain is it that a visit to the Riviera still lacks its finishing touch until one has had a peep at" Monte" not merely because of the beauty of the place either, but because the "world, the flesh and the devil" are so much in evidence (and it would be haid to know to which of the three to give the palm) that even the most hardened golfer may feel himself by compatison, quite a decent, and it may be even virtuous member of society." In Switzerland, Mr Crosfield is again on familiar ground, and his description of golfing facilities, climate and scenery, at such places as Maloja, St. Moritz, Zurich, Montreux, Montana, which boasts the longest course in Switzerland, closes finally at "lovely Lucerne."

A MESSAGE TO TEACHERS.

By Canon Morley Stevenson.

The current issue of "The Woman Teacher's World" contains the following contribution from the Rev. Canon Morley Stevenson, principal f the Warrington Train- ing College, under the heading " The Week's Message " :—lt is well for those who teach to realise the wonderful power of example upon those they teach. What we say has not so much effect upon children aa what we are. Every child is a born actor, a born imitator. Unconsciously, there passes from teacher to child a stream of god or a stream of harm. .

To a teacher who takes her vocation and work seriously—and I believe the great majority do —this is a sobering thought. We cast about to find what help; we can to make this great force of example potent for good. Can we do better than strange our thoughts under the old three-fold philosophical division —the true, the beautiful, the good ? 1. THE TRUE.

_ Every teacher must be a seeker after buth. She must find truth herself that she may impart truth to others. If the teacher is found to have taught what is net true, the confidence of the pupil is shaken, and much harm will result. It is no disgrace to say that we do not know --for a teacher cannot be a walking encycloprrdia - but it is a disgrace to teach either what we know to be untrue, or fiorn pride to be ashamed to confess our ignorance, and so to make a hazardous statement, of whose accuraf y we have no certainty. But more important than this is f( r the teacher to take and impart to others true views of the value ef time, of the use of money, of the sanctity of work, of duty, of filial honour and obedience, of character, of life. It is so easy for a child to mistake tinsel for gold. The teacher's longer experience and contact, with life has taught her the difference, and put it in her power to teach others. If the lamp of truth burn clearly in our own lives, there is no saying how its light ID ,y not guide the lives of ethers into the path of safety. ' 2. THE BEAUTIFUL. Beauty is another potent influence ; how potent has not been sufficiently rec sgnised. I know one school where the head has in the courEe of many years collected objects of beauty, which, with a self-denial worthy of all praise, he keeps in his school, instead of in his home. . .

In the s•me school illuminated quotations from great authors meet the eye at every turn. Such quotations as this, "Beauty, goodness, and hnowlcdge are three sisters, and never can be sundered without tears." Every teacher can do much to inculcate the love of the beautiful in Nature, in art, in literature, and in character. The love of Nature and its beauties will do much to stop the senseless, wanton destruction of flowers, trees,and butterflies, which we all deplore ' • the love of art will bring a joy:into child's life, and teach him many a lesson ; the love of literature will lure him from the coarse, debased, and polluting books which, unfortunately, are far too common; and the love of the beautiful in character will do much to mould his own in a right direction. Mr A Benson in one of his books says : "If people could live more in the generous visions of posts, if they could be taught to see beauty in trees and fields and buildings, I think they would be happier and betrer. Most people are obliged to spend the solid hours of the day in necessary work. The more sordid that work is, the more advisable it is to cultivate a perception of the quality of things. Every( ne has hours of recreation in every day ; the more such hours are filled with pleasant, simple, hopeful,beautiful thoughts, the better for us all."

3. THE G °or>. It is not necessary to say mut:ltt about this. Of all powers, the power of goodness is the greatest. All of us know someone of whom we can say, " He is a god man," and no higher praise can we give. Goodness consists not in visions and high-sounding words, but in the control of the life in the round of its daily duties and the little common things of the common days. A teacher's life is strenuous and exhausting. She is constantly tried by little, irritating pin-pricks. If through it all she can keep herself in hand, be patient, sympathetic, unselfish, and calm, she is bring- ing the principles of goodness into practical use in every-day life. " He kept his soul unspotted As he went upon his way, And he tried to do some service

For God's people day by day : lie had time to cheer the doubter

Who complained that hope was dead ; He had time to help the cripple When the way was rough ahead : He had time to guard the orphan ; and one day well satisfied With the talents God had given him, he closed his eyes and died."

===== 0002601_19120629_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
" I AM FREE FROM PAIN FOR THE FIRST TIME IN 10 YEARS." A MARVELLOUS iiiEPRESCRIPTION.

A Manchester man writes :—When I got that instant relief I told my wife that I would write to the papers so that others would know of it, if I was still free of pain at the end of the week. I have no doubt now that lam cured. Cured after suffering agonising pain night and day for ten years. What it would have meant tope had.l known of the prescription earlier. I am certain that it will cure any pain, whether from Neuralgia, Neuritis, Sciatica, Lumbago, Rheumatism, Headache, &c. No case could be worse than mine. The Examiner, Saturday, June, 29 1912.

newspapers bad told of an alarm of fire being given by a dog. That has happened within a couple of years at Stansfield Hall, Todmorden—the very house temporarily in the eccupation of Mr. B. S. Townroe, the prospective Unionist eendidste for the Middleton Parliamentary division of Lancashire. The dog was owned by Mr. John Barker, the lessee of the hall. In the small hours of the morning of the occurrence it began ti bark loudly, and ran to its master's room as if trying to attract his attention. It was first heard by Mrs. Barker, who awoke with a choking sensation and found the place filled with smoke. The Fire Brigade were summoned by 'phone, and the fine old hall was preserved from serious damage. LEAP YEAR, TOO! A solo contributed at the public,. meeting in connec- tion with the annual conference of the Independent Methodist churches reminded me of an incident narrated by an alarmed widower not many miles from where I write. He is a lay preacher of the Baptist persuasion, and his services are in great request by churches of his denomination over a pretty extensive radius. A while ego he accepted an invitation to preach at one of which a lady missioner had charge at the time, and at a preliminary interview in the vestry the visitor courteously gave her the option of selecting the hymns for the service. I happen to be well acquainted with the worthy man and had the story from his lips, and I may as well give the sequel in his own words. Said he: "She thanked me for the offer, and said she would avail herself of it to only a limited extent; in fact, she would choose but one hymn, and leave me free as to the remainder. Turning down the leaf to mark.,the place, she closed the book and handed it to me. I seiz^d an early opportunity to glance at the page thus indicated, and I tell you it gave me quite a shock whan I discovered the contents. What do you think the hymn was ?

JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

GROSS DARKNESS. The discussion at last week's meeting of the Board of Guardians revealed amazing ignorance in relation to the Counties' Band of Hope Union's scientific temperance lecture project. Just as strongly pre- judiced publicans and their fi bands imagined that the last piece of beneficent legislation for the protection of innocent childhood against the generally recognised evils of drink was an ()tavern med attack on their class on the part of a host of unreasoning fanatics, so some of our local Poor Law administrators are so ill-informed and behind the age as to fancy that a series of up-to-date addresses on physiology is deliberately and ingeniously designed by a b xly of unscrupulous teetotalers to further dishonest ends through the media of the elementary day schools! They evidently consider the specially trained and exceedingly careful and fair agent in the matter as an avowed opponent of the trade and all its works, incapable of any higher thought than how he may hit the enemy below the belt. Adverse critics who so regard the lecture scheme in question are strangely belated, for it has elsewhere worked with all-round satisfaction for quite a number of years. I know publicans by whom it is heartily commended. After hearing the earlier reports of their children viho have been present at preliminary lectures, they have allowed them to complete the course, and have been as proud as other rarents of the essays subsequently written by their youngsters. What better proof of the absolute fairness of the lecturers can the most fearful and exacting Guardian require?

TIM KING AND AMBULANCE MEN.

For the first time in its history, the St. John Ambulance Brigade was reviewed by the King in Windsor Great Park on Satur- day, when His Majesty passed in review a body of 15,000 men and women, voluntary members of a great and helpful army of workers. Two interesting displays, illus- trating the varied character of the work undertaken by the Brigade, were given. The first was a display of life-saving from shipwreck by members of the Tynemouth and South Shields Volunteer Life Brigade. The second display dealt with mine rescue work by members from various collieries, under the command of Mr. H. C. Chambers, district secretary, No. 5 district. A. repro.

duetion of a coal seam, with a low roof, under which a number of miners crouched with their picks, had been erected. There was a burst of flame, as if all explosion had occurred, and apparently a fall of roof. The miners were gradually overcome by poisonous fumes. Rescuers, wearing oxygen breathing apparatus, cleared a passage to them, and brought succour in the most realistic fashion. The Royal party showed the keenest interest in both displays, which were smartly carried out. The illustra- tion shows a group of Welsh miners, who took part in the colliery rescue work, while below is the canvas screen, beneath the roof of which the display was given.

SAFEST LOCOMOTION. It was noted in last week's " Examiner " that a witness at the Titanic inquiry gave it as his conviction that a voyage by a modern liner was the safest form of travel. I remember Earl Russell, in the " Inde- pendent Review," labouring to show that in the con- sideration of this question of the safest mode of locomotion, the train must be placsd first, and the motor car next in order. To be sure, two or threel years have passed since thus the noble lord contended, but there is a sense in which the article may for that very reason be held more interesting, and I may at least reproduce the introductory sentences. After observing that the legalisation of motors was but about a dozen years old in this country, and their practical use to any extent not more than half that, his lordship continued —" Although our highways are inferior, haphaz ird and badly engineered, and although the population that use them have not yet got out of the bad habits of tileepy centuries, it is already true ta say that next to English railway trains the motor car is the safest mode of locomotion. It kills fewer people per million miles run Ithan any other form of traffic, and as an engine of destruction cannot be mentioned in the same breath with the butcher's tart or the hooded van. Palatable or no, these facts must be swallowed and a period must be put to this idle talk of special danger to life land limb horn this motor car. Not less instructive is it to take 1,000 accidents at random and inquire minutely into the cause of them. In the vast majJrity of cases it will be found that the accidents are directly due to the contributory negligence of the victims."

CRIMINALS AND ANARCHISTS. The atistocratic writer's conclusion of the whole matter is really too delicious to be omitted. Ido not know if in the meantime he has seen fit to modify his views, but the arguments which he advanced and the sentiments which he expressed so comparatively recently strike one as decidedly out of date already. After complaining of the conduct of carters and drivers of other horse-drawn vehicles and of the behaviour of small boys in persistently getting in the way of that imperious monarch of the road the motor car, Earl Russell closes with this curious outburst : " All these people are criminals and anarchists; they have not recognised the obligation of a duty to their neighboors which is the condition of a civilised com- munity. They must be dealt with as criminals ; they must be, and on every moral ground they ought to be dealt with for endangering the life of a motorist and , their own with the same ruthless ferocity as the motorist encounters on the bare statement of a police- man that he endangered somebody whom he missed by half the width of the road. These people must be taught, and all users of roads and the public in general must learn that millions of the ratepayers' money are spent upon the roads every year in order to provide means of communica- tion from one place to another and not to provide places for gossip, playgrounds for children, or private yards to be m-nopolised by sleepy carters." So there !

AN ALARMING EXPERIENCE. The terrible accident of a week ago on the Lanca- shire and Yorkshire Railway occurred precisely three miles from the Rev. 11. Ogden's native place, and he must be be very familiar with the spot. I have myself long known it as one of the most dangerous curves in the kingdom, and a member of my family recalled the other day that I had repeatedly expressed my fear that it would one day prove the scene of a disaster. The cause of the shock iug mishap of yesterday week is avowedly • a mystery to the driver of the ill-fated express. The result of the Board of Trade inquiry cannot be anticipated, as the proceed- ings only commenced on Tuesday, but I once lived within a few miles of the spot and I observed the platelayers were everlastingly tinkering at the per- manent way just about there. It therefore seemed to me that trains ought never to be allowed to travel there at a high rate of speed. lam not aware that in the case of the one now referred to it was excessive, but generally speaking, familiarity breeds contempt. Moreover, in these " Get-there-quick " days, sacrifices are often made and risks taken in order to save a few minutes here and there, so that the ultimate responsi- bility is really with the public whom competing com- panies have to serve. OPEN-AIR MUSICAL FESTIVAL.

SUCCESSFUL EFFORT FOR THE INFIRMARY.

The open-air musical festival has now become a well established annual means of raising funds for the Warrington Infirmary. The credit for this successful enterprise—one of the happiest inspirations which ever occurred to the promoters of charitable effort— must be shared equally by the Workingmen's Com- mittee, who engineer the arrangements, and the members of these local musical organizations without whose co-operation it would be impossible to submit such excellent programmes. Sunday afternoon's festival was a distinct success from every point of view. Although heavy showers in the earlier part of the day gave rise to misgivings, there was a welcome change as the hour of the festival approached, and the sun shone forth so reassuringly that the park was soon thronged. Happily those pleasant conditions continued during the whole of the time the concert was in progress and the audience was therefore enabled to follow every item with appreciative attention. The programme pre- sented by Messrs. Crosfield's band and the Warring- ton Male Choral Union received careful and musicianly treatment. Of course, a choir is at some disad- vantage in singing in the open-air, for even a largo body of voices seems to produce a much lees impressive volume of sound than is manifest in a building, and it is also more difficult to bring out the details of expression and light and shade. We have never beard the choir—or its two separate predecessors— sing so well in the open-air as on Sunday, for the attack was crisp and clean and the entire programme was characterized by refreshing unity of purpose and a determinalion to got the best out of every piece. We were rather sur- prised to find such very old friends as " Crossing the I plains " (Price) and " Spartan heroes " (Protheroe) figuring in the programme—not that we have anything but admiration for them, but they have been beard so often in Warrington as to have become hackneyed. The wonder was that the choir sang them with such freshness. The "Hymn before action" (Walford Davies) went with fine verve and " When evening's twilight" (Hatton) was a veritable gem of harmony. Perhaps the most interesting w ark was that delightful glee 'Strike the lyre" (Gook) and the vocalists entered into the spirit of it with a thoroughuesss which resulted in a fine realization of the varying moods of the composition " Beleagured (Sullivan), " Come let ,us sing ' (Beal) and " Stars of the summer night" (Bond) were all nicely sung and well received. Mr. S. Hassidl was an able conductor and Mr. F. Atkinson played the pianoforte accompaniments. A special stand for the accommodation of the choir bad been erected at the expense of Messrs. Peter Walker and Son, Ltd, Messrs. Crosfleld's band acquitted itself with customary brilliance. The grand selection " Maritana" (Wallace) constituted the chief test of the all-round I capabilities of the band, and the favourite numbers I from this opera were given in a most finished and artistic style. " Eventide " (Sivi ft) and " The pilgrim's song of hope "(Satiate) with their choice variations, were striking features and the overture " Light cavalry " (SuPPe) was very brightly and accurately played. Mr. W. Halliwell conducted with his usual tact and skill. The collection taken at the gates and the small extra charge made for the use of the chairs near the bandstand raised total proceeds of £45 108. 21c1.—an increase of about on the sum received last year. Amongst those who took an active part in the promotion of the festival were Alderman Burton (Chairman of the Workingmen's Committee, who was unable to be present on Sunday, owing to absence from home), Mr George Walker (vice-chairman), and Mr J II J Hampson, the hard-working secretary of the Infirmary and hon. secretary of the Workingmen's Committee. Others in attendance included Alderman and Mrs Smethurst, Mr J H Smethurst (chairman of the Boyd of Management) and Mr W Daintith.

"GO ABOUT YOUR BUSINESS."

The old Temple clock in London bears a curious inscription, the origin of which is ascribed to a chance remark.

Some two hundred years ago a master workman was employed to repair and put in a new fac9 upon the clock. When his work was nearly done he asked the benchers for an appropriate motto to carve upon the base. They promised to think of one. Week after week he came for their decision, but was put off. One day he found them at dinner in commons.

"What motto shall I put on the clock, your Lordship ?" he asked of a learned judge. " 01], go about your business !" his honour cried angrily. _

" Aneveiy suitable for a lazy, dawdling gang!" the clock maker is said to have muttered, as he retreated. It is certain that he carved" Go about your business "on the base.

The lawyers decided that no better warn- ing could be given them at any hour of the day, and there the inscription still remains.

THE CAT THAT CAME BACK.

The story of a notable journey of a cat from Kingston, Surrey, to its former home at Glastonbury, in Somersetshire, a distance of about 133 miles,ris told by a correspondent in the "London Chronicle."

In April last a cat, mottled gray and described as half Persian in breed, was sent from Glastonbury, where it had been reared, to the matron of the Kingston Nursing Home. It was sent in a packing case drilled with air holes and obviously the animal had no opportunity of marking the points of its journey. Soon after arrival at Kingston the cat was lost and despite the most diligent searching, no trace of it could be found. The matron of the nursing home has now received a letter from Glastonbury stating that the cat has found its way back to its old home. It arrived emaciated and footsore and apparently had walked the whole of the 133 miles.

The letter describes the cat on its arrival as "a mere shadow of its former self" and expresses the belief that although terribly weak through its wanderings, it can be nursed back to a healthy condition.

to go, Perhaps it .is a Saturday, all spoiled by clouds and

rain— Well, then, would it be just the thing to fly away to Spain! All right, I'll go! Here's Irving's " Conquest " ready on the bbelf ; I lie and read —and soon in bright Granada find myself!

Sometimes I visit Arthur's court, and join the Table Round ; Again, with Mr. C. Columbus I am westward bound ; Sometimes it is that wonder book, the famed "Arabian Nights," And then upon my magic couch I take the strangest flights!

And that's the secret— don't you see ? I'm sure you never guessed That there were any such things now—enchantment and the rest ! Come up some time and try a trip —just now I've got

YOUNG ABSTAINERS' UNION. Visit to Penketh House.

On Saturday last the members of the newly-formed Warrington Branch of the Young Abstainers Union were entertained by Mrs. James Dodds, the generous and energetic president of the Branch. On their I arrival at Penketh House, the little members and the I committee were conducted by their kind hostess Ithrough the charming and extensive grounds, covering some 31 acres of well-kept lawns and beautiful Ewer gardens, the latter fast approaching their best bloom ; then through the orchard and well-stocked kitchen garden, both showing good prospects of a heavy yield. —After indulging for a short time in games on the lawn, the visitors were in fine form for the excellent tea which their hostess had so kindly provided. Then after further games and rambles through the grounda, the visitors thanked their hostess and reluctantly took their departure, thus bringing to a close a visit, the remembrance of which will take a long time to efface. It may perhaps be of interest to many to know that the Young Abstainers' Union is a temperance society for young people of the upper and middle classes. As the Union was founded in 1879 it is not surprising that on the membership roll there are now a large number who cannot claim to be called young any longer. The Union is unsectarian and non-political and amongst its patrons are to be found such well- known names as the Bishop of London, Bishop of Durham, Bishop of Peterborough, Bishop of Liverpool, Dr. Clifford, Dr. Horton, Dr. Jowett, Dr. Meyer, the Countess of Erroll, Lady Barlow, Lady Roberts, Sir T Vezey Strong, and Sir George Kekewick. The secretary of the Warrington Branch is Miss B. Ewart, 4, Winmarleigh-street, who will be pleased to answer all inquiries.

John O'Brien, Major Cross-street, Widnes, was on Tuesday charged at tbe Widnes Police Court, with having been drunk and disorderly and with assaulting Police-constables Rochford and Carroll ; and James Wilbraham, and Louis Wilbraham, father and son, Major Cross-street, was charged with resist- ing the polioe.—Police-constable Roohford said that at 11.15 last night be was with Carrol in the market square when he saw O'Brien and Louis Wilbraham lying on the ground in Alforde-street. COALYARD MANAGER SENTENCED.

Mr. John Chambers, book-keeper, of 22, Prestfleld- road, Elleamrre Port, was charged at St. Helens, on Monday, with embezzling £3 14s Id from the Wigan Coal and Iron Company, Mr. Allen, for the prosecution, said Chambers was appointed manager of the company's yard at St. Helens in July, 1909. He had to sell coal and E n ter particulars of every bit of coal that went out of the yard. and ta render accounts every month. Every- thing went on satisfactorily until February, 1910, when prisoner sent his statement. The company never heard Prom him again, and his books were found to be in a hopeless mess. He had received one sum of £3 14s id from Councillor Glynn which be had not accounted for. The total amount they had lost was £lB9 16s 4d. Prisonersaid he was appointed to open the place and start the business, and a great quantity of coal was stolen from the yard. He knew that the stock was getting short and he tried to put the thing right. He had to go out for orders and leave the place without anyone in charge. Chambers was sentenced to six months' impTisonment.
