SAD AFFLICTION DISPELLED.

Girl Victim of St. Vitus' Dance made Bonny by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills.

Four years ago, when details were first published regarding the affliction of little Miss Mary Burn, of Nottingham; the matter excited much comment. The child had been the victim of St. Vitus' Dance in a terrible form, but grew quite active and bonny as the result of her cure by Dr. Williams' Pink Pills. A visit to her home at 71, Holgate Road, Notting- ham, recently, disclosed the fact that Mary has grown into a well-developed, attractive girl of fifteen, bearing not the slightest trace of the nervous malady that once prostrated her. Her mother repeated the history of her girl's sufferings and cure in the following words: " Mary was a helpless invalid, stricken by one of the worst attacks of St. Vitus' Dance ever known. A numbness affected one side and then extended to her arms and legs ; in time she became unable to walk. First the rnus-lea of her face began to twitch, then her limbs. We had to hold her arms while we dressed and undressed her. The twitchirgs grew more severe, however, and her power of speech-left her,

Mary Burn.

WOMEN'S GHAT.

Royal Visits.—The King and Queen have still numerous society engagements to fulfil in London, before the season is over, but a break will be occasioned when on July Bth, their Majesties start on a week's visit to Earl and Lady Fitzwilliarn, at Wentworth Wood- house, near Rotherham. Differing from the Royal visit to South Wales this week, their Majesties will not attend any public functions in Yorkshire, but the King will take advantage of his visit to go over some of the great industrial centres of the West Riding, and also the show of the Royal Agricultural Society, which is this year! being held at Doncaster. The King intmded also to visit some of the chief centres of industrial life in Lancashire, but these arrangements have been deferred until their Majesties visit Lord and Lady Derby at Knowsley, next year. Later on, in .7nly, the , King is to attend a Thanksgiving Service at Winchester Cathedral, and His Majesty also has an engagement on the North-east coast, to open the new docks at Imming- ham. The Royal visit to Henley on the 6th, is a new and picturesque feature of the ar ason, and the King and Queen will proceed down the Regatta Course in the state barge. Ihe Draped Dress.—Ascot has set its seal of approval on the pallier, or, at anyrate, the new interpretations of it, and therefore we are told there is no longer any doubt that the draped dress has come to stay. There is, however, a prevailing impression that the new vogue was not a success at Aso rt. To many enlookers, thst ultra-fashionable racing festival, notwithstanding the brilliant weather, seemed only to emphasise the many disadvantages which attach to draperies, for the open- air summer frock. It does not, like the straight-line gown, suggest coolness. On this score, the most flimsy materials are employed, to give the desired dressy effect, but when the wind whistles through chiffon folds and raises them into the semblance of balloons, the effect is anything but graceful. It was said that some of the " hip paniers " seen at Ascot were made to come on and off, so that the dress could be worn either way. However that may be, wisdom dictates that we should reckon with the elements in choosing the open- air summer dress, for seaside, river, or garden party and this must necessarily mean the avoidance of draperies which can be agitated by rude breezes. Colour in Dress —Professor Stirling, in a lecture the other day upon Optical Illusions, dealt scientifically with the problem of colour contrasts and illusions, a subject which is also dealt with by Mr George A Andeley, in his little book " Colour in Dress." Every- one knows how black clothes deceive the ere and reduce the apparent size of the wearer, and it may be learnet from these authorities why this is so, and also the pre- cise effect of different shades in conjunction with the colour of the akin, eyes and hair. For the true type of the fair blonde there is only one generally improvit g hue, end that is green, inclining to the olive or sage green, because it imparts an agreeable glow of red to the skin, a deeper tint to the eyes, and richness and warmth to the hair. There is another colour which e ill simultaneously emphasite these features, but it should be modified in the case of the rud 'y blonde, for whom bluer, inclining towards the green and grey shades, are the most advantegeous. Cream-white, which is usually I considered a rate colour for all types, may be sad with I safety by the pale brunette, especially when a s ,elated with suitable colonrs, but brown of a kindly warm hue is, as a rule, her wisest choice. The lighter browi a. yellow, and deep gold colour are also sniteble for the florid brunette, constituting with the hues and tints in the skin, an agreeable gronp of barmonising colours. Chains Worn Again —Jewellery, like everything else, has its fickle changes of fest ion, and this year there is a return to the long chain of fancy beads, with a craze for the semi reecious stones. Red and purple stones ae intersper..ed with pieces of jade and turquoise, the latter often uncut. Amber beads are also in great request for long chains, which pass two or three times round the neck, and the beads of past genera ions are very diminutive, compared with the big globes that form the necklace of to-day. Bold effects, rather than beauty, in light and exquisitely delicate workmanship, are aimed at, arid wherever possible, the spice of Gov lt y is introduced. Some of the chains formed of pink- rock crystal and Chinese enamel, are 'Lung alternately eith carved peach stem s, the latter being impregnated with some exotic perfume, and these ornaments depend ' not so much on the costly materials, of which they ere composed, as upon their bizarre effects, for the knell. I bestowed upon them. The Cloak of Silk.—The modest dust cloak, as we used to know it, was a very pimple and unobtrusive garment, of doubtful cut and fit, and quite devoid of decoration. But this season's vogue for taffetas has changed all that; the dust cloak has passed its chrysalis stage, and emerged from it, like the butterfly, a thing of beauty and a joy. Probably the cool lied changeable weather of early June, which made some kind of wrap a 1 necessity, has helped in its development. Anyway, tbe alr at of silk is a feature, not only of evening, but of day wear, and it cannot be too ornate. Silk of all kinds, sometimes in a coarse cord, again in a fine grain, are chosen for the beautiful models, to be seen in the shops and warehouses. Sometimes it hangs loese, exposing the front of the dress, or it is fastened with large handsome buttons in front or at the side, but always it has handsome collars or revers of lace embroideries or other decoratir ns. 1 The Virtues of Casement Cloth.—No material for summer wear is more useful than casement cloth, and it is at the same time effective and economical. Thanks to the sunny window manufacturers experimented with it until they discovered a process of rendering it impervious to the most ardent rays of the hottest summer sun, and it, is no exaggeration to say that since it has been rendered unfadeable, it in more freely uses for dresses than for the purpose for which it was originally intended. The newest cloths are greatly improved, for they have now a wondrously soft silk finish, and are made iu the loveliest shades' and with embroidered collar and cuffs they make delightful frocks of the simple kinds. Or this material adapts , itself in its new guise to ruchings and frills, that are associated especially with taffetas. The Petticoat.—Small by degrees and beautifully less, may be truthfullt remarked of the undergarments worn by women since the silhouette became the "Ire- veiling mode. Now, however, petticoats are returning to popularity, but they are of the skimpiest and most clinging variety, innocent of ft minces and frills. Toey are usually made of pongee lass, or a similar thin material, and trimmed with insertions of delicate lace. Quite a new make has a top of a material as elastic as stockingette, that fits the hips without a shade of fulness, or the shadow of a wrinkle. Just above the hem there is a skimpy flounce of shadow lace, in some instances covered with fringe. There are intended for both day and evening weir. Dearer Footwear.—Although much is beard about the successful production of artificial leather and rubber, we are told that our boots and shoes are to cost us more. There is nothing astonishing in that in these days when everything is increasing in price, but a short time back we were informed that we should soon be wearing boots soled with a material prepared from seaweed, quite as good as the best leather. It has been tried and proved as a good substitute, but apparently not much importance is attached to it as a prospective means for cheapening the production of footwear. Some people theught that the greatly lessened demand for harness leather might have such an effect, but this it seems, is counter-balanced by the diminished number of horse hides, and the increased use of leather for upholstering motor vehicles. The larger reticules, which have taken the place of the " Dorothy" bag, have also greatly increased the demand for leather materials.

BUSINESS SECRETS.

MESSRS. CROSFIELD AND THEIR

EMPLOYEES.

In the Chancery Division, on Saturday, Mr. Justice Joyce heard a motion in the /union of Joseph Crosfleld, Limited, v. Frederick Wright for an injunction to restrain breach of a service agreement. Mr. Hughes, K.C., for the plaintiffs, said the defendant did not appear, but he had been duly served with the notice of motion. The defendant was in the employment of the plaintiffs under an agreement of service which set forth that he "shall not during tbe said term, whether he shall or shall not remain so long In the company's service, nor within two years after- wards, without the consent in writing of the company first having been obtained, disclose or divulge or com- municate to any person or persons whomsoever any Information whatever as to substances or articles in the manufacture of which Wright was specially engaged, or in relation to any other substances or articles of the company's manufacture." his Lordship : He is gone now ? Mr, Hughes : Yes. But the period was for the ten years of the service and two years after that. The service was terminated on the 20th April, 1912. All he asked for by the motion was for an injunction until the trial of the action, or two years from the date of the discharge. The defendant was not much more than a labourer ; he was not a skilled workman, except that In the course of the work he would acquire information about the plaintiff's process. The point was that they could prevent him going into similar employment during the speciflo term, but if they did that then they had to pay him £lO a year. That was to say, they compensated him to that extent in addition to what wages he might earn in any occupation he went into. , His Lordship: Your evidence is that your consent has not been asked. If!. Hughes said that was so. His Lordship granted the injunction asked for, with the modification that the limitation for two years was only for interlocutory purposes and without pre- judice to the true construction of the agreement.

The Examiner, Saturday, June, 29, 1912.

BAPTIST PROGRESS.

NEW SUNDAY SCHOOL AT GOLBORN E-STREET.

THE MAYOR AND STATE SUPPORT OF RELIGION.

Saturday s itnessed an important forward move in connection with Golborne-street Baptist Church. For many years the congregation has realized the need for more commodious/school premises, and now, after much self-denying effort, a building scheme has been Successfully launched. The foundation stones of a school which will meet every requirement on up-to- date lines were laid on Saturday by the Mayor (Councillor Joseph) and several prominent workers for the Baptist denomination. The complete under- taking provides for the re-building of the school behind the present church and an extension, including re-fronting, of the church itself. The site for the school is of a restricted character, but full advan- tage bas been taken of the area at the builder's disposal. A large I all will occupy the ground floor, accommodating over 300, a portion being capable of sub-division into seven class- rooms by m vable partitions. A young men's institute room, 30ft. by 15ft. Gin., will be placed on the first floor and there will be suitable accommodation for kitchen, minister's vestry, &c. The church front is to be rebuilt with new central porch and side staircases The matei bile for facing the front are Accrington facing bricks and buff terracotta. The contract also provides for re-ceiling the listing building and further accommodation at the gallery end of the church. The whole tithe buildings are to be heated by hot water and fitted with electric light. The total coat is approximately 350. The architect of the scheme is Mr W 11 Matley, 14, Ridgefield, Manchester, and the contractor is Mr G Wallington, of Warriogton. Saturday's ceremony was favoured with beautiful weather and there was a large attendance of members of the congregation and friends. 'I he Rev John Yonge offered prayer and the lesson was read by Mr E Wynne Jones.

minister of the Wesleyan circuit the Rev W A Beckett, wished them God speed in their effort and expressed the hope that the new premises would prove a centre of great spiritual blessing. Continuing, Mr Ogden said that unfortunately the two Baptist ministers could cot not be present. Mr Chedburn was away in South Wales and they all knew of the tad event which detained Mr Curtis of Hill Clifte. He had been extremely delighted that afternoon when one saw the realisation of one's hews and expectations for the little ones. He felt he had been somewhat aepaid for the drudgery and uncongenial requests he had had to lay before the various gentlemen of the county.flowever,whilst he had been going about amongst the good, bad, sod indifferent subscribers of the county he had been buoyed up by the thought that one was going to realise something for the children. Emphasis- ing the necersity for iefhtencing young people in the right way, the speaker said they might take a leaf out of the Roman Catholic book and adopt thoir meth° 'el without their marts. They got the child before it lisped and, given a sufficieet period in which to train it, were able to defy everyone. He trusted the new school would be an educational institution and that they would sae their children grow up in grams and wisdom. The teachers and all associated with the !wheel should see to it that they kept the children informed upon what were the nocessitits and rroblems of the day. They very often heard it proclaimed that the Church was out of the current of human affairs. (Hear, hear). If they were true to their history the young folk who went from their s-hool would go out fit to take their part in the great and strenuous battle which lay before then. A dirty, vile Tlem meant depraved children and morally and phyaio.lly depot ved men and women. ALEXANDRA DAY.

Wednesday was "Alexandra Day," and at ten million flowers were made for the countless points in the Metropolis fair ladies occasion by two hundred crippled and blind —including many of high degree—sold wild girls in Clerkenwell. England's first "Flower roses for the benefit of the charities in which Day" is likely to have many successors. Queen Alexandra is interested. No less than

The Rev. 11. OODRN (pastor), in an introductory seeech, said anyone who was acquainted with the work at Golborne-street would be ready to confers that they were sorely in need of new premises. (Hear, hear.) One generally heard when Acts of Parliament were being passed, that they were loins overdue. No statute In the country, was, however' more over-due than their new Sunday school. (Laughter and npplatiar). Plans were prepared so long as twenty-eight years ago but many and insuperable difficulties bad stood in the way of the realization of the scheme. They rejoiced that at last they were able to lay the foundation stone of more suitable and commodious buildings in which to carry on their work. Such a step forward was all the more necessary because of the gre,t position which the liapti.t denom- ination took in the life of the nation. Reviewing the achievements of the denomination, Mr. Ogden pointed out that in the world (,f literature they produced John Bunyan, in the world of preaching Charles Spurgeon and Maclaren, while one of their leaders was the pioneer , missionary of India and another did a great work in the ' opening out of the Congo. In the world of politics, everyone might not b 3 willing to own Lloyd George— (laughter and applause)—but the Baptists were proud to remember that he was brought up as a Baptist and was professing Baptist to-day. Tkey all knew the Baptist upbringing of Mr. Birrell and the work which his father did in the ministry. If the Lord Chancellor were true to his ancestry, he would ben strong Baptist. (Applanse.) With such noble traditions to uphold, It was only fitting that in the centre of Warrington there should be a building worthy of their denomintion and of its great work. (Applause.) He sometimes tried to analytic the public outlook in Warrington, for the reason that he happened to be born in one of thoso small towns in the West Riding of Yorkshire where, in a community of 25,000, they had seven Baptist churches, all hilecomtely storied with ministers and with premises suitable for the Leeds of the locality. It hat often been a puzzle to him that in a great town like Warrington there should only be one Baptist church—that was in the heart of the town itself, The future was, however, full of hope and they believed their denomination would be able to take a worthy place in the social and religious life of the town. When their new premises were completed they would satisfy the taste of the most fastidious Baptist and he esteemed it a privilege given by God that he should have been at Golborne-street when stlea an important work was commenced. In welcoming the Mayor, Mr Ogden said that although he was not connected with their denomination he was willing to place the civic and public spirit first and to help forward any noble work that was being attempted in the town. (Applause). The MAYOR, before laying the first stone, delivered a short speech. He hoped most fervently that the enlargement of the school and the remodelling of the church might be attended with an increased flow of spiritual good and that the church would strengthen its influence in the religious and social life of the town. He was reminded that many years ago he used to visit that Sunday school not as a scholar but as deputy public vaccinator, and even in those days he used to notice bow much the place required renovating and ventilating. It was the bounden duty of everybody to encourage an undertak- ing of that kind. In this age of materialism, when agnosticism we, rampant, it was the duty of all who professed religion to Light against the common enemy, and that could not be better done than by helping to build Sunday schools and churches and to encourage organisations which combatted sin of all kinds. Even from the sanitary point of view, he did not think it was by any means consonant with the will of God that people should assemble for public worship or instruc- tion under such conditions that they were being taught to heal their souls at the risk of damaging their bodily health. His Worship next made passing allusion ' to what he termed "weak points "in orthodox religion —not that they were wrong, but they presented grounds for attack by infidels or agnostics. lie thought the clergy should devote great attention to defending those weak points. Ile hoped that church, and all the other churches, would have an Influence amongst the poor. The rich also required influencing, but they were not so helpless in various ways as the poor. The churches that were poor and struggling ought to be helped more than those that were rich. Be could not help thinking ' that the religion of God ought not to go begging to anybody. It ought to be above that and, although it might be a strong statement to make in publio, ' he believed every section of organized Christianity • ought to be equally supported by the State. ' The Mayor then laid the stone and was presented ' with a Bible, suitably inscribed, as a memento of the occasion. _

Stones were also laid by Mrs. Watts (for Mrs. Johnson), of Manchester, Mrs Buckby and the Rev H Ogden. Mr. E. WYNNE JONES paid a tribute to the Rey. H. Ogden's exertions on behalf of the scheme and said he had in a few weeks collected over £lOO in the county. (Applause). The school children then took their part in the cere- mony by placing a number of bricks in position. PUBLIC MEETING.

_ In the evening there was a good attendance at a public meeting held in the Wycliffe Memorial Hall. The Rev II Ogden presided and was aupported on the platfdm by the Revs J E Roberta (Manchester) and ii V Thomas and Messre J W Soholetleld and E Wynne Jones. If a fox go up, be shall even " -^nk down their stone wall." Nehemiah was not 0.. atriot but a religious patriot. Besides being a nationalist, he was a religious zerlot and where others beheld a ruined Jerusalem, a city without hope, be saw the glory of Jerusalem returning. For such a reason the struggle with Ireland had been Eo intense. England took her cue from His Grace of Canterbury or the Memorial Hall, Farringdon- street Ireland turned to Rome. As Protestants we might deplore it, but we dare not, save at our peril, ignore it. Nehemiah believed that Jerusalem was not played out. Sanballos might jeer, but Nehemiah bad a faith that would not shrink. Unfortunately he bad to deal with a di s spirited people. All the heart had been taken out of them. They took all their punishments lying down, None but a religious zeahet would face such odds. Napoleon's marvellous campaign when be marched acrues the Alps with all its peril and daring, was easier because he had a buoyant army behind him. He led men who had faith in their leader and themselves Jerusalem was not played cut. Ridicule and jeers would not stay the woik, for behind was a leakier who believed in God and himself. Just now that Church is attemi ting a great task. To nisei £2,300 was a great undertaking for a fee people. If the Church was played out they were westing valuable treasure ; they were frittering away precious gifts. But was the Church played out? 1 hey were told that there w a a mighty unrest in the land, and the churches were in the cold. The bla-ue arts laid upon Lloyd George and his knavish tricks. But Lloyd George, great as he was, was but a production of his age. The open Bible, especially the New Testament, the songs and claims of Brotherhood had made such men p saible. 'I he Church had been their making. Before her was the print of her bands. Could the church ba played out when the little troll angel aajutant melted the heart of criminals ? Cc old her work be over whilst such men as Canon Barnet used ? Will Crooks might have be+ n nurtured in a Workhouse, but he was a child of the Church. Where- ever there was the Living, there was the Church. The agnostic flouted their works ; the sceptic jeered at their labours. lathe 18th ci n ury the Church was said to be dead. Her critics had moulded to dust. The work of the chu•eh remained, and the church will live so long as she knew her living Master was with her.

GRAMMAR SCHOOL SeouTs.—The scholars of the Lymm Grammar school held their annual athletic spot ts on Saturday last. Being such a pleasant afternoon there was a large attendance to witness a successful and popular series of contests This year owing to the increased number of contestants, there were some exciting finishes witnessed. If it did nothing else, the mile race proved that in the future the school should be the . fortunate possessor of an excellent runner in Master E Davies. Though the youngest mem' er in the event he led for the first half mile, and even when over- hauled by several of the other runners he kept going right to the end of the course, for which be was awarded a special prize. Much amusement was afforded the spectators by the boys in the Composition race, whose amateur efforts to master the joinery and sewing tests which the events supplied, proved too much for the restraint of mirth. A new event in the form of a Girl's Morris Dance with violin accompaniment by Miss Anita Pearce and Mr W Burrell, was an acceptable change. The various conditions which hive to be fulfilled in order to lay claim to the title of champion athlete have I been more than met by the excellent performances of A Tomlinson. The rival houses into which the scholar's are divided, namely, " Domville " and " Warburton," have various contests during the year for the challeni e cups and bat that have been presented to the school I y local ladies and gentlemen. This year the "Doraville,' partisans have been remarkably successful, capturing all the tropies. Lyrum Subscription Band provided a musical programme whilst the sports were in progress. During the afternoon the visitors were entertained by Mr and Mrs C H S Willson to a dainty tea served on the lawn in front of the school. At the conclusion of the last event the psizes and cups were presented to;the successful contestants by Mrs C J Miller. The headmaster, Mr C H S Wilson in a short speech thankedfall those who had assisted in making tne sports a success. Be expressed his delight at the improved nunner of entries and said that he hoped that on future occasions they would be able to add medals for swimming to their prize list. Mr G L Welford also addresser.' the gathering and said that the scenes of that afternoon nad made him feel quite youthful again. He said that Mr Willson's remarks about swimming medals reminded him of the fact that perhaps at no distant date we should have open- air swimming baths in tLe lake.

Dr. J. F. M'Cleary, editor of " Public Health," the official organ of the Society of Medical Officers of Health, has been agp, inted principl medictl either to tie National Insurance Con in'ssioners.

Dr. George Frederick M'Cleary is medical officer for llnmpt teed, and former/y held a similar position at Battersea, where bin wr rk in connection with in'ants' milk depots will he long remembered. He gradit ited at Cambridge in 1892. takieg his degree as a Bachelor of Medicine in 1818, end I).i:sowing a doctor of public health lu the hollowing year, and a Doctor of Medicive in 1901. RIFLE CLUB.

The Appleton team journeyed to ermston on Monday evening for the match. URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The monthly meeting of the Lymin Urban District Council was held on Thursday evening in the Council Chamber. Mr. W. G. Groves presided and there were also present:— Mr T H Warburton (vice-chairman). the Rev G B Thurston, and Messrs G L Welford, J H Holt, Davies' R H Gotobed, Mr W Mullard (clerk end co [lector', Dr De Jong (medical officer of health), D Mort (surveyor) and W LDonalds•n (gas manager). MR. LOCKYRRS HEALTH.

In answer to the deputy-chairman the clerk announced that Mr Lockyer, one of the members of the ConLcil, was very much better in health. HABITATION CERTIFICATES. COUNSEL'S OPINION TO BE OBTAINED. At a meeting of the "toads Committee held on June 20th it was resolved that it be a recommendation to the Council that Mr. Ridgway, solicitor, Warr ington,be instructed to obtain Counsel's opinion as to the Council's right to refuse a certificate of habitation when plans had been passed subject to tbe owner of the proposed building giving an undertaking to give up a piece of land without payment when called upon by the Council, and when such condition had not been com- plied with;also on the general question of the Council's rights to rk quire land to be given up by persons intend- ing to build, for purposes of widening the road on any of the following roads :—Cherry-lane, Higher-lane, Burford-lane, Mill-land, Stage-lane and Rush Green. The minutes were approved. _ ISOLATION HOSPITAL.

The Building and Fire Engine Committee met on lune 20th, and it was resolved thatlessrs Holt, GrOVf a and the Rev G B Thurston be appointed a sub- committee to go into details with regard to the pro- posed steam disiufector, and wash house at the Isolation Hospital. The minutes of this committee were also approved. THE NEW STABLES. CARPENTERS' AND JOINERS' GRIEVANCE. A letter eas read from Air E Moston, branch secre- tary of the Carpenters and Joiners Association, stating that enquiries glad to note that the clerk had made some e re the Council stables contract, which, be was ihreie wass sorry to say,was not altogether satisfactory.He had been instructed by the members of the Branch to again state that the contract had not been carried out according to the advertisement for tenders in the case of the joiners emplojed on the job. Ile asked to be allowed to intro- mice a deputation on the bubject, and the Council granted the application.

- - THE TRAINS. MINIMrM ACCOMMODATION AND MAXIMUM DISCOMFORT.

ktr Gototed raised the question of insufficient railway accommodation and said there Was a very noticeable lack of accommodation on the trains running between Manchester and Lymm and especially on the Saturday trains. Only last Saturday the 135 train from Oxford-road to Lymm consisted of four small coaches and a guard's van and the coaches were chiefly used I y South Junction passengers. Every compartment was crowded to excess and a number of people had to travel in the guard's van. He did not think it was a satisfactory state of affairs at a time when Lymm itFelf needed every assistance from the railway company to encourage residents in the district. He moved that the clerk be instructed to write to the Passenger Superintendent asking for better arrangements. At the present time it seemed to be the minimum of accomodaticn and the roaximuT of discomfort.

----- Sir. belt, in seconding, said he quite agreed with Mr Gotobed hut he would like adding to the resolution that the 12.25 train from Manchester to Lymm be altered to 12.15 to enable business men to come to the village for lunch and be able to go back by the 1.38 which was impossible at the present time. He also suggested that the first stop on that train be at Broad - heath, as that would stop South Junction passengers from getting in. , Mr Gotobeds' resolution with Mr Holts addition was unanimously carried.

Padgate.

WALKING DAY.—The village was en fete on Saturday last which was the date of the annual Walking Day celebrations. Beautiful weather prevailed and served to assist greatly the fine appearance of the Fchoole taking part. The amicable arrangement obtaius of the schools taking tUTTIS in the leading of the procession. This year (owing to their having acceded to the urgent request of the C.E. schools to allow them to walk first last year as the State Church in the Coronation proces- si n) the Wesley ans were in the van. Led by the Salvation Army Band, they left the Wesleyan Church at 2 30 under the leadership of the Rev E Morgan and the officials of the school and accompanied by Mr William Bennet and party and Mr James Gandy and party, who drove in their own conveyances Immediately following came the children of the Cottage Homes, and then the Echolars of the Established Church led by the band of the Cottage Homes and accompanied by the vicar and officials and a detach- ment of the Church Lads Brigade. The members of the Risley Independent Methodist Sunday School brought up the rear. In each case gaily decorated ,lurries carried those scholars too young to walk the whole I distance.. With respect to the decorative work in the procession the last named school easily bore off the palm. Chief among tiese was a mail cart in blue and white colours, the work of Mr. J. Houghton and a set piece on a litrry representing by a cross " Rock of Ages "to which a girl clung for refuge. In the Church of England section a mailcart arranged by Mrs. IVebh in pink rosettes was worthy of great praise while one in the Wesleyans by Miss L Mellor had not only a pretty effect, but the additional merit of being decorated with natural roses the charming beauty of which no artificial work can approach. After the singing of the National Anthem the scholars proceeded to their own schools where substantial teas rapidly disappeared. Later, in the various fields the usual sports and games were indulged in until darkness closed a day of glorious weather. _

WEsi,EyAN CHURCH ANNIVERSARY.— On Sunday last the annual chapel sermons were preached by the Rev A H Walker. 8.A., of Manchester,to large and attentive congregations, who greatly appreciated his ministra• tions. During the morning service the augmented choir rendered the anthem" I love the Lord "(Sullivan). A musical service was held in the schoolroom in the afternoon, Mr T Duckworth presided in the regretable absence of Dr. Naden who had been announced. The choir gave a floe rendering of the last portion of Maunder's cantata " Pardon, Penitence and Peace." Mr Nathan Cook sang" Lead kindly light." Gounod's motet"Gallia" was sung with great effect by a specially selected portion of the choir assisted by several well- known local singers. At the evening service a selection from the cantata, given in the afternoon was sung the soloist being Mr Warburton.

TANNERY BAND'S SUCCESS AT BLACKPOOL.—SeveraI well-snown bends competed in the brass band contest in the Tower Circus, Blackpool, on Salad ty afternoon, prizes to the value of .£5O being tiered. The test piece wes a setting of Verdi's " Higoletto," arranged by MeFsrs Wright and Round, of Liverpool, and plajed for the first time in competition. Mr J 0 Shepherd, the musical director of the Royal Court Theatre, Liver- pool, was the adjudicator, and he made his awards as follow :-1, Wineates Temperance (conductor, Mr J A Greenwood) ; 2, Black Dike Mills (Mr J A Greenwood); 3, Foden's Motor Works (Mr William Halliwell) ; 4, Longridge (Mr William Heap) ; 5, Penketh Tannery (Mr J A Greenwood). Winwick.

PARISH Coirmr., MEETING.—A meeting of the above Council as held on Tuesday evening, Canon Penrhyn in the chair. Others present were :—Messrs Wallace, Alker, Hambleton, Longton Shaw, and W Cooper (clerk).—The allotments question was again raised, and the correspondence between the Commissioners and the applicants, ace. pting the site, was read.—Mr Longton proposed and Mr Alker seconded, that the rent be paid half-yearly and in advance —Mr Hamhleton said that he thought that if that were passed it would be considered a slur on the character of the applicants and he moved an amendment that the rent be paid at the end of each halt-year's tenancy, but be could not find a seconder. Tenders were invited for the fencing of the allotments. The question of a burial ground was discussed. The Clerk had received a reply to his letter to the Town Clerk of Warrington, asking for reduced fees at the Cemetery for Orford people, saying that this could not possibly be granted.—Mr Hambleton said that this was a very sore point with the inhabitants of Orford, and it was useless for the Council to consider making a burial ground in Orford. The question was of vital importance to the whole district and it should be gone into at once.—Mr Hambleton then brought forward the state of Longford Brook, the condition of the main road and the sides. It as resolved to write to those who polluted the brook, and to ask the Urban District Council to join with V e Parish Council in complaining abaut the state of the road from Winwick Cnurch to Looglord. FIRF. BRIGADE SI:MEM—The National Fire Brigades Union (North-Western District) held their annual tournament at Fleetwood on Saturday. There was a record entry, firemen assembling from all parts of Lancashire and Cheshire. '1 he weather I eing all that Could be desired, everything passed oil without a hitch. It was a great credit to the enterprising secretars, Captain Jarvis, and his hard-working officials. An official lunch was provided at the Nlount Hotel, after which "Long fiervice " Medals were presented to Chief Officer H E Parker (20 years service) and Second Officer Nowell (10 years service), of the Winwick Asylum Fire Brigade. Captain the Hon. H Arden-Baillie-Hamilton in making the pre- sentation stated that be had known Chief Officer Parker during the number of 3e1515 that he (Captain Hamilton) had been connected with the fire nervice, and could speak se to the retard and esteem in which he was held by his fellow officers in the Fire Brigades Union. He sincerely hoped Chief Officer Parker would be spared many years to wear the coveted honour bestowed upon him that day. Chief Officer Parker then received the medal amidst many congratulations, and much cheering. During the afternoon the Asylum Brigade were once more successful in winning the Harrison Challenge Shield, and four silver medals presented by Surgeon- Major Harrison for the hose-cart drill. This shield has been won by the Asylum Brigade four times, viz :- 1908, 9,10 and 12. The brigade also won the Haddington Cup and the gold medal in 1911. The members of the Brigade who composed the competition team were : Messrs A Dugdale, 0 Dawson, L L Lee, J Suttie and Woods.

rEARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

LORD WOLMER'S BET.

LACKING ULSTER TO BEAT ALL IRELAND. Ajoint demonstration was held in Garswood Park on Saturday hy the Conservative organisations of the Newton division. Old English sports, a whist drive, dancing, and political addressee formed the programme, thepithief speakers being Lord Wolmer, M.P., and Mr. P. K. Glazebrook (MP., for South Manchester). Mr., Arthur Smith (chairman of the association) presided. Lord Wolmer said the enthusiasm of Welsh and English Churchmen bad struck terror into the hearts of the Government, and that was why they dare not go on with the Disestablishment Bill, although they had introduced. it. Be they Churchmen or Nonconformists, as Christians they would never see one of the great churches torn down and plundered to the extent of two-thirds of her endowments as long as they bad strength to resist it. Anot;er great fight was on the qcestion of Home Rule. and they declared that they would not hand over their Ulster fellow-countrymen to the men who had been the foes and enemies of England. They in Lancashire knew what good fellows the Orangemen were ,and what stuff they were made of, and that they would not be trampled upon by John Redmond and Co. They were told that Ulster must be left under the power of Mr. Redmond and the Nationalist party. Even if that happened he would not mind betting his last sixpence that the Nationalist party and all their followers would never be able to "lick " the Ulster men. When a man was hit in the eye he did not as a rule like the man who hit him, and that was why Mr. Asquith and Mr. Lloyd George aid not like Mr. Boner Law, because he told them the truth straight to their faces in the House of Commons. The Conser- vative party would not try to pull down the ancient Constitution and destroy the Church. They would devote their attention to some of the more important problems. They were the practical common-sense party, and they were going to win all over England as they had won in Newton. (Applause). A resolution expressing support to Mr. Boner Law in opposing the Government proposals and endorsing his statement that the best means of solving the labour troubles would be by a change of the fiscal system and Imperial preference, was coried. •

NEW ASSISTANT MEDICAL OFFICER.

meeting of the Health Committee on Tuesday evening Dr. Geo. Wm. Nit-Id Jr.seph, M.D., B.Ob. (Honours), D.P.H. (Liverpool) was unanimously appointed assistant borough medical officer, vice Dr. Hutt, who has taken up an appointment at Brighton. Dr. Joseph, who is 32 years of ago, is an "old boy" of the Bcteler Grammar School, Warrington. On leaving there be studied medicine at Liverpool University. In 1907 he graduated Bachelor of Medicine and Surgery with honours,and since then has obtained the degree of Doctor of Medicine and the Diploma of Public Health. During his under- graduate career be was awarded 10 medals in the subjects of the curriculum, including one for hygiene and public health, and also the Kantbach medal for pathology and bacteriology. He was elected Senior Lyon Jones Scholar, and was granted the Gee prize for diseases of children. He has held resident appointments at the Royal Infirmary, Liverpool, first as ambulance surgeon and then as house surgeon to the special department for eyee, nose, throat and ear diseases, and for two years he was engaged as a-sistant with a large general practice in Liverpool. During the past two years he has acted as resident medical officer at Mill-road Infirmary, LiverpoJl.

At the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday before Councillor H Rober.s (in the chair), Councillor C Jackson Holmes, Colonel Edelsten and Mr T Locker, Thomas Molyneaux, 73, Dailam-lane, was charged with having assaulted Margaret Plinston, a barmaid at the Royal Oak Hotel, Bridge-street, on the 19th inst.—Mr Steel appeared for the prisoner and pleaded guilty. The Chief Constable said that on the evening in question the prisoner was with his brother in the Hotel and about 10.30 they had an altercation and the prisoner was struck at by his brother. Miss Plinston went in between them with the intention of separating them. and while doing so the prisoner, it was alleged picked up a pint pot and threw it at his brother, but it missed him and hit the barmaid on the bead with considerable force. She was rendered insensible and remained in that condition for a considerable time. She was immediately attenthd by Drs. Langdale and Fox and was still under a doctor although it was now reported that the injuries were not of serious nature. . .

Mr Steel said the prisoner desired him to express his great regret for what had taken place. Ths brother began to have an argument with the prisoner about his mother. He commenced calling her names and the prisoner took his mother's part and his brother struck him in the mouth, making his lips bleed. Prisoner then picked up the jag with the intention of throwing it at his brother, put Miss Plinston came between them and was struck. The prisoner was prepared to compen- sate the barmaid to the very utmost for the injuries she lad received. The prisoner was a married man and had been in one situation thirteen years. The prisoner promised to pay the doctor's bills. The prisone- was ordered to pay the doctor's bill and 41s. costs, including 20s. as compensation to Miss Pliuston.

PLOODED CELLARS.

A complaint having been recently made by Mr G Norman to the effect that his private cellar drains at 70, London-road, were frequently "back-filling," causing a serious nuisance on the premises as well as the adjoining shops. He alleged that the sewer was responsible, it not being of suflicier t capacity. lhe Clerk stated that the Local Government Board had received copies of the correspondence and asked for the observations of the 1, ural Council stating that the sewer in London-road was sufficiently large for the ordinary requirements of the district. The only time it 'vas overcharged was when there were heavy storm; and even then it did not affect the drains except those connected with cellars. In reply to a suggestion that a FAR trap gully would meet the requirements Mr. Norraan had replied that he had fixed ball trap gullies 1 in connection with the drains, and they had not had the desired effect. As to the statement that the sewers were sufficiently large for ordinary requirements, he would like en explanation es to why Ellesmere-road and Fairfield-road were partially under water in times of rainstorms. It was decided that the Surveyor inspect the property and the drainage and report to the committee.

===== 0002601_19120629_art0129.txt | included=true | included =====
. - DANGEROUS ADVERTISEMENT HOARDINGS.

"They are a menace to the public safety." was Mr Brown's description of the advertisement hoardings at Twenty Steps Bridge when this matter was raised in , the meeting. Within recent weeks he had seen two or three verb narrow escapes from accidents there. Be did not know who was responsible but it was time the Council threw off any responsibility with regard to the hoardings and expressed an opinion on the danger the hoardings were. If someone was killed the coroner would want to know why the matter had not been taken up before. The Chairman said the place vas a death trap ; for motorists coming along Fairfield-road could not see what was coming up from the the town. He bad noticed motors, hundreds of times coming up without giving the slightest warning. Mr Brown : A motor nearly took the bridle off a horse there the other Saturday. Mr Whitley : It is not the only place. A resolution was passed to the effect that the ntten- tion of the Manchester Ship Canal Company be drawn to the advertisement hoardings at Twenty Steps Bridge

and that they be informed that the hot r iings prevented a sight of the cross-ro,ds by drivers of motor cars and other vehicles, and that, in the opinion of the com- mittee they were a source of danger to the travelling public and should tre removed.

ATTEMPTED IMPOSITION.—George Fenton Wigley, 6, Forster-street, Formby was charged on remand with attempting to obtain food by false pretences from Ann Newns on June 16th. Prisoner pleaded guilty. The Chief Constable said the prisoner was seen at 7.15 p.m. on the day in question by Detective Reddington, in Parker-street. He watched prisoner and saw him go into White-street and Parker-street and look up at several windows and ultimately go to Mrs Newn's house. The officer was informed by Mrs Newrs that the prisoner had asked for board and said he would require lodgiegs. In reply to Mrs Newns he had said be was working for the Cheshire Lines Committee. It appeared that on Saturday night the prisoner went to the Cheshire Lines Station and asked the foreman porter to give him a job, but to told him he was unable to do so, and that the man should inquire at the goods department on Monday. Wigley had been well connected and had been a worthless sort of a son.—The prisoner said be thought there was a chance of getting work, and he could not stay out all night. Wigley, who had been in custody a week, was discharged on promising to !Kota the town. TuurtsoAv.—Before Mr W Roberts (in the chair), Mr A J Jolley, and Dr Naden. SERVANT IN TROUBLE —Martha Barrow, a domestic servant, was charged with stealing a lady's gold dress ring, value £2, the property of Mrs Widdop, of 3, Henry-street. The Chief Flonstable stated that for the past live weeks the prisoner had been in service with Mrs Widdop at the house in Henry-street. About 3 30 the previous day the prosecutrix saw the ring safe on the dressing table. On going upstairs a short time afterwards she missed it. The maid was suspected and Mrs Widdop ultimately found the ring in the girl's coat pocket. She left it there and went for P.C. Cameron. When the officer arrived the girl said she knew nothing about the ring. During the time Mrs. Widdop had been out for the constable the ring zbad been put back on the dressing table. The girl's mother had seen her wearing it and had been under the impression that Mrs Widdop had given it to her. The Chief, continuing, said he did not think the girl had intended to keep the ring, as she had worn it and put it back where she had got it from. Barrow was bound over for 12 months nnder the Probationers Act.

Barr proved the case.—The Chief Constable said that damage to the extent of about £l2 bad been done by boys in the same district quite recently. Doors had been removed and frames had been taken out of windows. It was not the mere fact of the lads playing football in the stre-t but strong action must be taken when property was damaged and filthy language was used by the boys. The language of some youngsters in our streets was positively disgusting.

ANGLING.

WARRINGTON WORKINGMEN'S ANGLING ASSOCIATION. (N.A. CENTRE). The coarse fish have not shown up in great quantities as yet and the anglers hare not turned 4,Ut in great numbers. Perhaps the holidays coming close together has a great deal to do with it. However, Hartford. Chester, Mickle Trafford and Norton, were visited, and the only fish to go on thescales was a gudgeon from Norton, this being the second from Norton this turn. Members tay that when gudgeon come in this length in ony qu tntities it is a sign of Acton Bridge coming on, as they generally come from that quarter this time of the year. The secretary has had word from headquarters that Acton Bridge water is quite free from pollution at present. It has been specially examined and members going that way should spend half an hour or so to try for the roach and eel.

===== 0002601_19120629_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
HARTFORD.

The Weaver was fished hy a larger number of Northern anglers than usual, the Saturday contest being the drawing card. Most of them stayed over-night to again indulge in the sport on Sunday. There was plenty of sport but few specimens, and the river is not likely to go wrong like last season owing to drought.

===== 0002601_19120629_art0133.txt | included=true | included =====
NORTON.

This spot is quite the favourite for locals who want to have a day canal fishing. The largest roach taken was one about 9oz. with the worm. A gudgeon tipping the scales at loz. Mrs. was the only decent fish taken during the day, although small ones were about and had to be returned to the water.

THE GOWY.

Here again sport was very bad. Eels were taken but not in any quantities. One or two dace and small roach was also landed but that was about all. Anglers present said it was quite mn oft day.

eItrSTER. The Dee was tried last week-end by one of our local anglers who knows this stream quite well and bw reports that there was nothing doing both up by Eaton Hall or by the swims at the bottom end, the coarse fish neing enthely off feed.

NOTICE. Members who fish the Norton length must produce their card when challenged by the bank ranger. If they have forgotten or lost it or cannot produce it when asked for they must pay their sixpence or cease fishing without protest as it is their own neglect, and the bank ranger is not supposed to know who are members and who are not. A.S.

St Maryi-won by 11.

Child Drowned in Canal.

An inquiry into the circumstances surrounding the death of Hilda Whalley, aged two years daughter of Frederick and Mary Elizabeth Whalley, of 33, Crossley-street was conducted by Mr F A Jones (deputy coronet), at Warrington on Monday. The mother stated that on Tuesday she sent the cbild out in a go-chair with its sister aged seven years, to buy some butter in town. She told the. eldest child that when she had made the purchase she must go into the Park with her sister. Later in the day she was informed that the child was missing, and her daughter told her she took her sister to her aunt's at Latchford.—The, aunt of the child who resides at 21, Myrtle-grove, Latchford, said the children came to her house about 4.15 p.m. The elder sister came into the house and left the deceased outside. Witness went to the back door to see the greengrocer, who had called, and she made some purchases from him. On calling for the younger child, she could not be found. She (witness) searched the neighbourhood and went on the canal bank, but could not fi ,d the child.— Joseph Beddard, a member of the 1\ arrington Grap- pling Corps, said be recovered the body from near Black Bear bridge on Thursday.—A verdict of" Found drowned" was returned.

THE INSURANCE ACT. WARRINGTON COMMITTEE AT WORK , The Scheme in a Nutshell.

With the approach of July 15th—the date when the Insurance Act comes into operation— the various local committees are getting their machinery in order and making. preparations to work the scheme in the most efficient way. The Provisional Committee, appointed to adminis- ter the scheme in Warrington, held its first meeting at the Town Hall on Wednesday even- ing, when Mr. G. M. Robinson attended on be- half of the Insurance Commissioners to direct the preliminary business and give a concise explana- tion of the Act. The constitution of the corn- mittee had been decided by the Insurance Com- missioners, after consultation with such represen- tative bodies as the National Conference. of Friendly Societies, the Federation of Trade Unions, the Insurance Companies, and the Dividing Societies. These organisations. toge- ther with the Town Council, were asked to nominate representatives. There still remain to be elected representatives of the medical pro- fession and some additional members Selected by the Commissioners. The full committee will consist of forty members. As at present consti- tuted the committee includes '2l representatives of insured persons, eight representatives of the Town Council, anti three members appointed by the Commission. The meeting was presided over by the Mayor (Councillor Dr. Joseph), and the keen interest which is being taken in the scheme was evident from the large attendance. I The first business was the selection of one re- presentative of small local friendly societies. The names submitted to the meeting were those of the Rev. Father Wilson, Mr. E. Plinston (secre- tary of the Trades and Labour Council), Mr. C. Dukes (president of the Trades and Labour Council), and Mr. W. Bibby (secretary of the Friendly Societies' Medical Association • it was pointed out that the gentleman selected need not necessarily be connected with a friendly society. Councillor Roberts. who proposed the nomina- tion of the Rev. Father Wilson, urged the im- portance of every section of the community being directly associated with the working of the Act. At present the Roman Catholics were not repre- sented on the committee. The friendly societies as such were adequately represented, without pressing unduly for further representation to the exclusion of assistance and guidance from other sources, which might prove of great value if they could only secure it. Father Wilson represented a large section of the populace which was inter- ested in and connected with friendly societies which were not specially represented, while other friendly societies were very largely specifically represented. Alderman Bennett seconded. He said they would not get the best results out of the Act if

they did not regard it as affecting the interests of everybody. The committee ought to be more ' or less a microcosm of the whole community. He realised that the friendly societies should have a preponderance of representation, but if they took too narrow a view they would not be working the Act to the best advantage. On every ground Father Wilson would be a most suitable representative. The Mayor said that while cordially agreeing with everything that had been said about Father Wilson, he thought it might be more suitable to elect the rev, gentleman as a representative of the deposit contributors.

Councillor Roberts: I am told there are ten societies specially connected with the Roman Catholics of Warrington, and that they are not directly represented at all. On a Tote being taken Father Wilson was elected.

The meeting then proceeded to select two mem- bers to represent deposit contributors. The Commissioners recommended that one of these representatives should be a lady. _ _ _

the names proposed were those of Mrs. J. E. Wright, Mr. E. Plinston, and Mr. W. Bibby. A vote resulted in the selection of Mrs. Wright and Mr. Plinston.

Mr, A. C. Binks inquired whether it was the intention to hold meetings of the committee weekly, fortnightly or monthly. The Mayor : That will be determined later on. The Deputy Town Clerk (Mr. A. T. Halls. way) : It will depend upon what work you have to do.

It was decided to hold the next meeting on Thursday, July 11th, at 7-30 p.m. THE ACT IN A NUTSHELL.

Alderman Bennett said it might be some help if the representative of the Commissioners would give the meeting some idea of what their duties would be, and the work involved. Mr. Robinson said that before the first meet- ing of the committee full information in writing would be given to the members, as to their duties and powers under the Act. Those of them who were members of societies would, no doubt, almost have slept on the Act—(laughter),--but some might have carefully put it away and thought very little about it. The Act would come into force on Julv 15th. It was compul- sory on certain employed people, that compulsion being effected by making TRH EMPLOYER IN THE FIRST ESSE SOLELY RESPONSIBLE for the payment of the contributions. He might, of course, recover a portion from his workmen. en there were some voluntary contributors— persons who were not employed by others, but who yet might take advantage of the Act. Tak- ing it very broadly, the normal contribution would be for a man sevenpenee a week and for a woman sixpence. Those contributions were paid by the employer purchasing stamps at a Post Office and placing them upon the contribution card, which the insured person would bring to his employer weekly. or at such other intervals as he paid wages. At the end of the quarter the insured person would pay the.card into his society or to the Insurance Committee, as the case might be, and when he had paid in thirteen stamps he had paid as good als thirteen sevenpences, or, in the case of a woman, thirteen sixpences. The Insurance Committee or the approved societies -would exchange those stamps for cash. The Post Office had got it all, of course. and the whole of the money would be placed in a Na-

tional Health Insurance Fund in Londona upon which insurance committees and approved socie- ties would draw to enable them to pay benefits.

THE BENEFITS were five i number. First of all there was medical benefit—attendance by a doctor and medicine from a chemist. The idea was to- have a panel upon which each doctor in the town might place his name, and the insured persons would choose from that list, at certain periods. which doctor they would have .to attend them. The same process applied to the chemists, of whom a panel would be formed. The Insurance Committee alone would administer the medical benefit. The second benefit was the sanatorium benefit treatment for tuberculosis in sanatoria, dispensaries, or ,at home. That, again, was wholly in the hands of the committee to adminis- ter for all insured persons. Then there was the maternity benefit of 30s. upon the birth of the child of a member. The sickness benefit would be nominally 10s.- per week for 26 weeks in the case of a man, and is. 6cl. for 26 weeks- in the case of a woman, the amount being lower for those under 21 and over 50 years of age. That was followed, if illness continued, by a disable- ment benefit, which was 55. per week in the ease of both sexes. That continued so long as illness continued, even to the age of seventy. At that age the sickness and disablement benefit ; ceased, and the contributions also ceased to be ! paid, but the remaining, benefits went on through- ! out life: ,The administration of the benefits was as follows :—First, the approved societies ad- ministered the cash benefits of their members— ! that was the sickness, disablement, and mater- nity benefits. The Insurance Committee admin- istered the remaining two—the medical and sana- torium benefits. As to the DEPOSIT CONTEIErTORS,

who paid their contributions through the Post Office, they would be persons who had not been able. owing to health, or character, or neglect, to get into an approved society. The Insurance Committee would administer the whole of their benefits, not only the medical and sanatorium benefits, but the cash benefits also. The prime business of the Insurance Committee was to ad- minister the sanatorium benefit. That ,

The Examiner, Saturday, June 29, 1912.

GROUP AT THE OPENING CEREMONY. Standing: Messrs. P. Delaney jun., (ton. semetary), E. Lloyd J( nes (chairman), A. H. Crosfield, P. Peacock, P. M. 011%er anct G. Toulmin. Seated : Mrs. Osborne, Mrs. A. H. Crcsfield and Ms a Crosfield.

away to attempting to grapple with too many cues of unfair hours of labour in indnstry.Keep the thing within manageable limits." They stuck to that policy and put iron and steel in the forefront of that movement. They also got evidence from other trades and one of the most striking facts was that in the

DIG PROTECTIONIST COUNTRIES though, with the exception of the wealthy States of America, the wages were admittedly smaller, the hours and conditions of labour were worse than in England. At the prasent time Eetween 30 and 40 per cent, of those engaged in the iron and steel trades in England had the benefit of the eight hours' day, and the percentage was increasing. In Germany, although over 1)0,000 men were employed in those industries, only 2 per cent enjoyed the eight hours day. The remainder were all working 12 hours a day,and a great many of them seven days of 12 hours per week, with a 21 hours' shift once a fortnight. He knew some provision was made for rest during working hours inside the works, but the hours were thoroughly and radically . bad. (Applause.) If they took the United States or America, the steel Corporation employed about 200,000 hands, and right through that industry the twelve hours day was the rule. All they were even aiming at to-day in the States, with their dry and wearing climate, was not a Saturday half holiday and a full Sunday's rest, but only one day's rest aiweek. Emphasising the need for international action to secure the universal adoption of the eight hours' day, Mr Crosfleld pointed out that its operation in some places had already meant a wonderful increase in productive power. If, however, son.e sacrifice would be compelled in some directions by that reform, it was only fair to the individual nation as to the individual employer, that the change should be inter- national, so that no section should be placed at any disadvantage. (Applause.) When they saw production in all the main industries of the world increasing mare rapidly than the population, one felt that there was a well-grounded hope for the increasing prosperity and comfort of the average worker. (Applause.) People might point out that with all our increasing prosperity there was a great deal of labour unrest, and ask if that did not point to lack of a

PROPER DISTRIBUTION OF WEALTH. He admitted that a better distribution of wealth was one of those things which had got to come and it was one of the proudest achievements of this modern democratic Government that real and practical steps had been taken in that direction. (Applause.) If employers had voluntarily made concessions to meet the advance in the cost of living in the last 10 years, instead of waiting for them to be forced at the point of the bayonet, we should have heard a great deal less about labour unrest. But it was unwise to measure the progress of democracy by observations only extend- ing over a decade ; lor real wages from 1850 to the present time had advanced from 40 to 70 per cent.—a fact which was eloquent testimony to what Free Trade had assisted in doing for the country. (Applause.) Referring to the

INSURANCE ACT Mr. Crosfleld said that three or four years would not have elapsed before they would place that measure on a level with old age pensions—he was not sure that they would not place it ahead of that beneficent scheme. They would undoubtedly marvel that we had zone on o long without national insurance. (Applause). Mr. Urostield made a passing allusion to a reterence in a Warrington Conservative newspaper to his "inefficiency" as a public speaker. Ile would remind that journal that

PEOPLE WHO LIVED IN GLASS HOUSES ought not to throw stones. (Laughter). The chief ambition of the "Warrington Observer" and its contem- porary the" Warrington Guardian "at the present time seemed to be to sing the praises and report every syllable they could get hold of from Mr. Harold rnith. (Laughter.) He (Mr. Crosfield) had read some of Mr. Smith's speeches, and anything more thin and superficial, anything more unfortunate than the state- ment of impossible assertions which he found in them, it would be difficult to come across. (Applause.) Mr. Crosfield concluded his speech by reiterating his earnest approval of the granting of a measure of self-govern- ment to Ireland, and expressed the hope thatthe junior Liberals would assist in putting an end to thepilly nonsense and the chimeras which had misled English- men for too long over the settlement of the Irish ques- tion (Applause.) On the motion of Mr. P. H. BROWN, seconded by Councillor HAWTHORN, a vote of thanks was passed to the speakers and to Mrs. A. H. Crusfi:ild and Miss Cros- field.

Mrs. CROSPIELD briefly responded. During the evening refreshments were served, the catering having been unaertaken by members of the Women's Liberal Associntion, under the direction of Mrs Osborne.

Wycliffe P.S.A. Society. Owing to the counter-attraction of the musical festival in the park there was a poor attendance on Sunday, presided over by the president. Mr. J. Travis of Widnes gave the address upon "The study of the Bible." There were he said, too many, especially young men, who did not give the Bible a moment's study. They were satisfied with the teachings and the opinions of others. They were missing the great privi- lege of seeing the beauties of the Word, and its many grand pictures. Mr. A. Healey led the prayer and Mr. McEachren also took part in the meeting. One new member received a hearty welcome. The brethren wished to send their best wishes for the speedy restora- tion to health and strength of Mr. Henry Osborne, their honoured treasurer.

CAME INTO FORCE AT ONCE

when the Act was put into, operation. ,With regard to the remaining benefits, there would in each case be a wait of at least six months before they came into force. The committee would have placed in their hands before July 11th a memorandum as to their powers and duties in relation to the administration of that particular benefit and their resources for that purpose. They would have no power to build sanatoria; that would be done wholly by local authorities and persons, and .t 1,500.000 had been provided in a recent Budget for that purpose. That money would be distributed among local authorities and persons. and was to be used for building grants. It would be intended for all tuberculous persons —not insured persons alone, but the whole population which came under the rule of local authorities. The work would be part of a county borough .or county scheme: Those schemes were now being prepared, and he understood the

===== 0002601_19120629_art0143.txt | included=true | included =====
WARRINGTON CORPORATION WAS ALREADY AT

WORK ON A SCHEME. Jr. that matter the insurance Commissioners and the Local Government Board worked hand-in- hand. Locally, in their relations to those schemes. the Insurance Committee should work hand-in- hand with their own local authority, and so prevent overlapping as far as possible. They had power under section 60 of the Act, with a view to such, co-operation, to invite the medical officer of health for the borough, with the consent of the Council, to attend any or all of their meetings. A scheme would have to be prepared as to sana- torium benefit, the building of sanatoria, and as to what the committee were prepared to advance for that benefit. The amount outlined in the Act was is. 4d. per insured person for that pur- pose, but a penny of that might be retained for investigation purposes by the Government. so that under the Act the Insurance Committee would be allowed is. 3d. per head per year. They probably would not get that. (Laughter.) Dur- ing the first half year things were rather difficult, and they must therefore look upon their arrano- - during that period as purely temporary, and take great care in the expenditure of their money. They were not a local authority; they had no call upon the rates, and if they outran the constable they would be in a rather serious position in their second year. The medical benefit, which the committee would be called upon to administer. vroild not come into force for six months. During that time negotiations were pending between the medical men and the Insurance Commissioners, and he feared they could only await the result of those discussions. As to the deposit contributors, the committee would have to administer the whole of their bene- fits. They were practically their approved society, and it would be necessary for them to have rules for the administration of those benefits, just like a friendly society, a union, or an insurance com- pany. Another matter the committee would have to deal with was the collection of certain statis- tics as to the

===== 0002601_19120629_art0144.txt | included=true | included =====
HEALTH OF THE COITSTY BOROCOH,

particularly in order to enable some classifica- tion to be made of the unfortunate deposit con- tributor class. The deposit system would only last for two and a half years, and during that time every committee in England would be col- lecting statistics in the hope that some classifica- tion of that unfortunate class would be made and something more be done for them at the end of that time. With regard to finance, the speaker urged upon the committee the duty of exercising the utmost caution in their expenditure at first, as they anticipated the amount for administra- tion would be abnormally small during the first six months. They would want some money to go on with, so the Commission would advance them a certain limited sum for their immediate necessities. The committee's tenure of office was rather undecided. They were elected under ,sec- tion 78, which gave the Commission power to elect insurance committees in case of difficulties. It would, however, be for six months certain, and they might possibly be in office for twelve months. The report of the recent

POOR LAW COMMISSION contained a hint as to what the duties of such committees might become: Up to the present those who had been administering friendly society work had been wholly concerned with cash payments during sickness and its cure by the provision of a doctor. Those who were meni- bers of local authorities had been pretty nearly concerned with the prevention of disease. Now they were to combine, as far as they were will- ing, and take a hand with the whole of the three things which were necessary when a man came into distress. The Poor Law Commission Report did suggest that it was desirable that the charitable and philanthropic organisations should join together in a town and co-operate more closely with the local authority and othe'r public authorities, and it looked as if in the Insurance Act was the first indication of the adoption by the various Governments of that new view of the civil life. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19120629_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
APPROVAL OF SOCIETIES.

Tne Commissioners desire to correct a misunder- standing which appears to exist in many parts of the country. No Society need suffer in membership or otherwise by reason of the fact that it is not approved by July 15th. A Society may be approved at any time.

The Commissioners are daily receiving fresh appli- cations for approval which must be dealt with in order, and provided a contributor has a Society to which he can hand in his card on or before October 15, he will not suffer any loss or harm by the fact that the Society of hi a choice was not approved on July 15th. LOCAL SOCIETY APPROVED. The Commissioners have now rldded to the list of Approved Societies the Hope Collecting Approved Society. As will be seen from our advertising columns this is a purely local Society, managed by local men. and as such ought to receive the consideration of all our readers.

JUNIOR LIBERALS.

OPENING OF A NEW CLUB AT WARRINGTON.

SPEECHES BY MR. A. H. CROSFIELD, AND

MR. P. M. OLIVER.

The Junior Liberals of Warrington, whose irdreased activity has been one of the most gratifying recent developments in progressive polities in the town, are now able to beast permanent quarters of their own. On Thursday evening the admirable club premises which have been secured in Horseurark et-street Chambers were formally opened by Ms. A. H. Crosfield and the occasion was naturally ene of great interest—a red letter day, in fact, in kcal political history. The rooms, v, hik h have been very tasfeitilly furnished and !quipped, include reading, Willard , and games rooms, and a secretary's office. The read- ing sip games rooms can, when necessary. be joined ibto one fir meeting purposes,. There is a splendid brlliard table and the premises thrcuOiout e replete wien every convenience. The assomation is greatly indebted to Mrs. John Crosfield, Mr. and Mrs. A. H. There was a good attendance at the opening ceremony, Mr E Lloyd J.,nes (ch,irrean f the Association) presiding.
