The CHNIR3IAN raid that dty was a red letter day in the history ot art Ington Young Liberalism. (Hear, bear). Acknowledging the %skiable help which had been fotthcoming from men.bers of the Crosfkld family, he said the town was greatly indebted to them, socially, commercially and politically. (Applause). The future of Liberalism in Warrington depended to a great extent upon the Organizing ot the young life for the party. They wanted to create in the juniors apolitical faith, tor it was that which was lacking in young men to-day, and they must alini inspire them to put that faith into action. Voting Liberalism should be the negation of laziness. It sriould be an active, living force, making itself felt in the town. The true purpose of that club was to give to its members a political education and thus make safe voters. The ttefurtu Hill now before the House of Commons would put hundreds of young men in Warrington on the register and the result of the next election would depend cpon how they dealt with the young men' (Aoplauset. Mr. DELANEY read a telegram from Mrs. John Cros- field regretting that, she could not he present, and wishing the club every Success. Mies CROSFIRLD accepted on behalf of Mrs. John Croafleld a handsome bouquet handed to her by Phyllis Jones.

Mr. A. H. CROSNIELD then gave a short address. Referring to the recreative side of the club and, in particular, to the facilities which it afforded for billiards, he recalled the fact that sixty or seventy years years ago his father and his uncle George Crosfield were closely associated with Cobden in the great movements of reform of the time. In between their political dis- cussions they occasionally indulged in billiards. One evening his uncle George, who was pretty useful at the game, gave Cobden a thorough beating. At the end of the enconuter Cobden, trot the least bit crest- fallen, turned to him and said " What a deal of time you must have wasted at this grime" (Laughter.) Contiuuing. Mr Crosfield arid he sincerely hoped the billiard table presented by Mrs. Cro*field, his wife and his sister, would be the means of a great deal of healthy recreation and pleasure to the members of the club, but he also hoped they would bear in mind that humorous remark of that great man Richard Cobden, and not waste too much time at the game. He believed in games being kept in their right place and in the exercise of a right sense of proportion. (Gear hear.) Therefore, while one would value a great deal of healthy recreation in that club, he looked forward quite as keenly and earnestly as the chairman to the greater uses which that club might have in the political life of Warrington. When the electors of Warrington sent him to the House of Commons half a dozen years ago, be went there baying been trained up a strong and advanced Liberal. He could assure them that the experience be had in the House and the opportunities one had in London of getting into touch with most of the advanced lines of thought of our time, certainly helped, if that were possible, to strengthen his convictions. (Applause.) He hoped that club would be a means of stimulating the whole of the progressive forces of Warrington, recognising as they must do, that politics in these days were more than ever, as regarded home affairs, the politics of social reform and realizing that social reform spelt nothing less than the gradual regeneration of our whole population. (Applause.) Mrs. A. H. CROSFIELD, having commended the optimism which characterised the Junior Liberal movement, said they must not allow their optimism to blind them to other things. She would recommend them in their work not to trust to flashes of inspiratien but to steady, persistent, continuous effort. Not only in politics but in all phaees of life, even including recreation, the same principle mutt apply. She hoped the comfort of those rooms would not tend to obscure the real object of the association, but that the members would be encouraged to still greater efforts for the progressive cause. (Applause) Nom Delaney then presented a lovely bouquet to Mrs. A. H. Crosfield. Miss Crosfleld was the recipient of a bouquet at the ' leinds of Mrs. Hoyle. In the course of a speech Miss CROSEIELD also emphasised the practical educational side of the club's work. he was delighted to see they hada photograph of Mr. Lloyd George on tt'e wall of the reading room. She hoped the junior Liberals who had seen how the women Liberals had come forward and bow Mrs Osborne had helped in the arrangements for that day's ceremony,would follow in the footsteps of , Mr. Lloyd George, than whom the women bad no better appreciater' and realize that the best thing of all was that Liberalism should be shared together by the men and women working hand in hand and side by side. She firmly believed that the home politics of this country would benefit enormously if a limited measure of women's suffrage, as Mr Lloyd George proposed, was granted in the Reform Bill. (Hear, bear). Mr P PEACOCK (chairman of the Liberal executive), said those associated with the senior organizstion were ' looking forward to grest things from the junior Liberals. He believed the juniors were going to stir up the senior associatien so that there would he such I work from the two of them and also from the Women's Liberal Assnciation that Warrington would be a really live place for Liberalism in the future.lt was a diserace that any member but a Liberal should be returned to represent a commercial town like Warrington, which had such a large working class population. He hoped the Liberal Government would see their way to enable women to have more direct share in the affairs of the I country. (Applause). Mr P M OLIVER expressed the hope that those magnificent rooms would be a great spur to junior Liberalism in Warrington. (Applause). The CHAIRMAN proposed a vote of thanks to the members of the Crosfleld family for their generosity. Mr 0 TOIMMIN in seconding, said he hoped the

Junior Liberals would tackle one or two of the new Leagues the Tory party had brought into Warrington. One League, which was rather paradoxical, called itself the Unionist Labour Party, and advertised in the Press that its officials were all trade unionists. To him it appeared that a man to be connected with the Unionist party could not very well be a trade unionist in the strictest sense of the word—(hear, hear)—that was,if he agreed with all the principles of the Tory party, for be (the speaker) noticed that several leaders of the Tory party had declared that if they got into power they would repeal the Trades Disputes Act and one or two other Bills that affected trade unionists. The Unionist ' Labour Party was, in his view, a bogus affair. (Applause.) The speaker created some amusement by remarking that the clock in that room was put down in the estimates as a good English clock, end he hoped Lady Greenall would take notice of that fact. (Langhter and applause.) The vote was heirtily passed. Mr. cRC SFIF.T.D, in returning thanks, assured the gathering that Mrs. John Crestleld took as deep an interest as ever she did in the progress of Liberalism in Warrington, and in all that concerned the welfare of the old town. She recognized in that development of an active movement by the Junior Liberals a force that would make for good report and good influence in the locality. (Applause.) In conclusion, Mr CIO field con- gratulated the junior Liberals on having as chairman a man like Mr. Lloyd Jones, who was not occupying his important povition its Pi mere figurehead, but who was a living force, full of enthusiasm, courage and energy. (Applause.) CONCERT IN IIIE CO-OPERATIVE HALL.

OLIVER. fie saw no reason why Liberals should not conquer Warringt n for they were infinitely superior in men and measures to the Conservative party. In the course of a contrast between the personnel of the respective parties he said that Lord Haldane, who bad just given up the helmet and put on a full bottomed wig, had during hi tenure at the War Office done one of the bigrp-st things possible for Liberalism, because in the face of great difficulty he bad maintained our

TRA DITION.A T. VOL UNTA RY SYSTEM of soldiering against that of conscription. (Applause). Lord Hastings had objected to Mr. Lloyd George being on the Front Bench of the House of Commons because be was the nephew of a cook—(laughter)—the person who contributed more to the happiness ofthe community than anybody else. ( Hear, hear and laughter.) We used to be told by a C diservative poet (Mr. ttudyard Kipling) that "Dukes' sons, cooks sons, and sons of belted earls " were all on a level ; but that was when we all bad to pay, pay, pay, for the Boer War. However, now that people were coming into I their rights of citizenship the Conservative party drew a line or demarcation and stid to the privileged classes" Sit thou on my right band and the rest of you go tO the bottom of the table." (Laughter and applause.) Perhaps it was because Mr Lloyd George wtts the nephew of a cook that he realised more than anybody else the necessity for having uhtaxed food. Coneequently, £21,000,000 had been raised last year in extra taxation be means which did not adversely affect the populace as the Tory method of putting a lu per cent.tariti on foreign food- stuffi and goods would certainly have done. (Applause.) There were only two constrnctive Or destructive measures which were advocated by the Ccnservative party—Tariff Reform and the amendment or the repeal of the Insurance Act. As to the former it was " All things to all men." and consequently nothing to any man (Laughter and applause). Parabling a story Mr. Oliver said that the Protectionist party in Warrington were being HAUNTED BY A GHOSTLY CLOCK. —(laughter)—and it might even be that the clock, which was made in Germany, appeared to their diseased imaginations, a coffin which was made in Broughton. (Loud laughterj. They were being hunted over hill and dale from Warrington to Walton and might finally expire at a place, which was very appropriate for the obsequies of the Conservative party,— the door of the public house. (Laughter). The Liberal Government had now provided for those who met with accidents in their work, with sickness, with unemployment in some trades, and with inevitable old age. They had thus provided for the misfortunes of life and DOW they had to raise the standard of life. It was going to be a difficult thing and some might leave the party, but others would join them. They purposed facing social problems and injustice would have to be removed. At present, in the towns, , industry was burdened with excessive rates.l Everywhere, in town and country there were r inadequate, insanitary and wretched houses and they had to be dealt with—and it was the Liberal party who had to do it. (Applause). When the Franchise Bill had been passed, for the first time at the next election progressives would be able to fight privilege on an Equal footing and then when the Liberal Govern- ment was again returned, they would be able to pursue their social policy. (Applause). Mr. A. H. CROSEIELD, who had a rousing reception, began his speech by wishing Mr. Oliver well in the plucky, uphill fight he was making in a neighbouring Tory constituency. (Applause.) Heproceeded to touch , upon the increasing reality and value of politics in the daily lives of the people, and his allusion to social reform led him to explain leading features of the work of the International Association for Labour Legislation in which he has taken a close interest for some years. In connection with the movement to establish an eight hours' day in continuous processes, a conference of representatives of many countries was recently held in London. Before that meeting took place he had the great adv.utage of spending many hours with some of the FINEST LEADERS OF THE TRADE UNIONS of England. He had in his time been privileged to , meet men of a good many types both in commerce and in politics, bat fairer-minded men—men with a more statesmanlike eye for what was practical and fair to both parties, be had never come into contact with than those representatives of the workers. Emmanuel (Reformed) Church

Sunday School' Anniversary Services. These were held last Sunday, and were of a highly encouraging nature. At each service the Sunday School choir sang special hymns, uhich were sweetly rendered to the enjoyment of the congregation. At the evening service the choir rendered the anthem "Judge me 0 God," and Mr. McArthur sang "When I survey the wondrous cross." These contributions to the service were much appreciated, and will not be soon forgotten. The sermons were preached by the Rev W T Jaye, (curate-in-charge) the subjects being" The Child Mind," " Beautiful Garments" (children's sermon) and "Love for tbe child." The dominant note throughout was the problem of how the Church should deal with the spiritual needs of the children. The preacher expressed the view that the requirements of the children load been too lightly considered in the past, while at the present time, in the attempt to make up for laxity in bygone years, there was danger of too much experimenting. The gravest menace to child life was the lack of godli- ness in the home : to those who had practical know- ledge of the young, this lack of godliness, and want of home restraint, appeared to be responsible for much of the leakage in the Sunday School just at the age when spiritual correctives were most needed. And consider- ing the question from inside the Church, the Church would never be equal to the task of training the young until it was recognised that it was essential that teachers in the Sunday School snould be consecrated to the work. The Master's " Loves t thou me " ? must be answered in the iarrnative ; then came the call to service—" Feed my lambs "—but not until then. St. John's Presbyterian Church. School Anniversary. The Sabbath School Anniversary was held on Sunday when the Rev W Reid preached morning and evening. In the morning Mr Reid spoke to the children on "kindness to animals,: quoting the story of " Androcles and the lion" and pointing to God's care for life in its lower forms and His appointment of man as its Saviour. To the parents and friends he spoke on the pious home aud religious customs in and amid which the child Jesus was reared, His appropriation of these customs BB His life became enlarged, and the additional ones He formed in later life. The expres- sions of life consecrate or desecrate our surroundings which in turn make their contribution of life or death to the soul .in the afternoon M r.Arthur Mounfleld spoke of Rome's great idea of carrying her civilization throughout the world, of the Roman soldier living only for his Emperor and taking the sacramental cup in which he pledged his life to the interests of the Empire of the hard training be endured, of his slrvices in Roman Colonies such as Britain, and of his undivided devotion and service. This type of man Paul gloried in and influenced so that Christ became known through- out Cmsar's palace. Speaking to the children in the evening of the prophets' school in Gileal and the loss of the axe head in the Jordan, Mr. Reid referred to the consciousness of straitened circumstances in life, and the efforts made to develop thern,of the attempts often made by youth in this direction without regard to the practical wisdom that age and experience can furnish,of the danger threatened to str)jective and objective life by looseness of habit and how the greatest denuncia- tion of evil is to be found in example rather than precept. To parents and others be spoke of youth as the period of life best suited to aid God. Being the critical and dangerous period, that best suited for acquiring and retaining impressions, and that most valued by employers, all went to prove that the chief concern of parents and teachers should be to enlist youth's powers in the service of God. On Monday evening some 28 prizes in book form, and 23 certificates and parchments awarded by the Sunday School and the Synod for merit in scripture examina- tion held in March, were distributed by Mrs. Ferguson of Stockton Heath. A children's choir rendered speciai music, solos and duets being rendered by Miss Winnie Bennett and Miss Ethel Hardcastle. Mr. Lewsey led the children and the combined choirs,and Mrs. Snailham presided at the organ.

The Halifax Clorporation Tramways Committee have made a trial trip by motor-'bus from Halifax to Mount Tabor and also to Siddal to ascertain whether those places could be provided with a motor-'bus service.

COUNTY PALATINE OF CHESTER. NOTICE IS HEREBY GIVEN, that the next GENERAL QUARTER SESSIONS of the Peace for the COUNTY PALATINE OF CHESTER will be holden at the CASTLE OF CHESTER, in and for the said County, on MONDAY, the Ist day of JULY, 1912 at Half-past Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon, when the Grand Jury will be empannelled. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Petty Sessional Divisions of Broxton, Chester Castle, Dares- bury, Eddisbury, Nantwich, Runcorn, Winsford, and Wirral], forming, the Chester District, and the trials of all prisoners committed from the last-mentioned Divisions, will take plaoe at such Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Chester District must be in attendance at the Castle of Chester, at the opening of Court on MONDAY, the Ist day of July, 1912, t _ Half- past Eleven o'clock in the Forenoon. THE COURT will be Adjourned to the COURT HOUSE, KNUTSFORD, on WEDNESDAY, the 3rd day of July, 1912, at 10 o'clock in the Forenoon. The hearing of all Appeals arising within the Petty Sessional Divisions of Altrinoham, Bucklow, Dukin- field, Middlewich, Sandbach, Congleton, Northwich, Prestbury and Stockport, forming the Knutsford District, and the trials of all prisoners committed from such Divisions will take place at such Adjourned Sessions. The Grand and Petty Juries for the Knutsford Distriot must be in attendance at the Court House, Knutsford, at the opening of the Court on WEDNESDAY, the 3rd day of July, 1912, at 10.30 o'clock in the Forenoon. REGINALD POTTS. Clerk of the Peace for the County of Chester, Chester, 17th June, 1912. DUBLIN ALSOa v E TEDCASTLE LINE. S.S. CAUSEWAY HOTEL. ANNUAL 1301VLINU MAIN. A howling main in connection with the above took place on Saturday. CONSOLATION COMPETITION. The match between the Liberal Club and King and Queen was the only match played in the semi-final on Saturday and the Liberals gained a magnificent victory of 44 points on the Bewsey-street green. The other match between Cemetery No 2, and Penketh Park was played on Tuesday evening and resulted in a win for the homesters, Cemetery of 48 points. -- LIBERAL CLUB V KING AND QUEEN. B Webb lei off well for the Liberals when he defeated A Jolley by 21-17. The game extended over 31 ends and was a very exciting one. .1 Monks (King and Queen) defeated S Riley by 21-14 in 27 ends. Riley was a beaten man all the way but pulled up somewhat in the last few lengths. -- H Cook (Liberals) turned the tables when he disposed of F Clare by 21-14 in 26 ends. At one time the Liberal had a lead of 14-3 but Clare added eleven points before the conclusion of the game and lost by seven chalks. As was expected A Gleave won his individual game. He was up against W Ashbrook and had little difficulty in winning 21-15 after 28 ends had been played. One of the best wins of the match for a Liberal player was the one of J Firth who scored 21 in 22 ends. to his opponent's II Walker eight. Firth was the best man all through and thoroughly deserved his victory. However the Liberals received a shock when W Ashton was beaten by R Fogg by 21-8. 'Null said. J Davies (Liberals) and T O'Brien (Liberal) were both successful in their games and defeated S Green, and W Locker 21-13 and 21-9 respectively. • --- W Taylor (Liberals) had hard lines in his game with 1 Makin for after having led up to the 17th end he eventually lost by a couple of points. He led D-18 at the 26th end but however he failed to add another chalk and in two more ends Makin was home. It Hitchmough was in his glory on the splendid green on Saturday and had little difficulty in disposing of J W Brown by 21-10. The game extended over 22 I ends at all of which Hitchmough had been leading. -- W Daintith slackened off to an enormous extent when be met W Taylor (King and Queen), for after having led 12-4, 14-5, 15-13 and 17-15, he eventually lost by two points Taylor pulled up fine in the last ten ends and played exceedingly well for his win. -- However the Liberals will fight bard to enter the final when they play at the King and Queen next Saturday. CEMETERY HOTEL, No. 2 V PENKETH PARK. Although they only won three individual games the Penkethites ran so close to their opponents that the w advantage for the Cemetery was only one of 48 points. -- H Rathbane (Cemetery) made short work of 3 Finch, for the villager only recorded 10 points in 23 ends. R young %Cemetery) beat H Bowyer by 21-17 in a game lasting 29 ends. Bowyer led 17-14 at the 25th end but this was his last score and the Cemetery man had little difficulty in gaining his majority. --- T Gregson (Penketh) was one of the few men on his side to win and win he did for he gained his 21 in 21 ends whilst his opponent, It Knight had only recorded six chalks. -- W Shaw was not in such good form and lost to It Brobbin by 10-21 the game occupying 22 ends. W Swanton (Cemetery) was put on his mettle by Joe Bowyer who although he only recorded 18 chalks put up a good fight as will be seen front the fact that the game took 32 ends to decide. lt was only on the last three ends that Swanton got away properly to win. J Foster (Cemetery) lost to J Bowyer by 18-21 after the game had lasted 28 ends. At the 22nd end Bowyer led 18-12 but Foster added six in the next six ends to his opponents three. No doubt the Cemetery men will be put to the test next Saturday for the Penkethites are terrors on their own sward. The return matches in thecompetition will be played next Saturday. BRITISH CROWN GREEN ASSOCIATION. LANCASHIRE V. DERBYSHIRE. Lancashire gained a magnificent victory over Derby- shire on Saturday for they won both matches, the one at Salford by 100 points, and at Whaley Bridge they

won by 74 points never losing an individual game in both encounters. -- Warrington can boast a fair number of County players and although there were no Warringtonians playing at Salford, we had four representatives at Whaley Bridge and they, gave very creditable performances. The best win of a Warrington player was that of the popular Whi tecroaq player &hart Glesve, who disposed of E Dodd (New Mills) by 21-13. A Scott was also successful in defeating R Dewhurst (Mellor) by 21-19. A similar victory was also gained by .1 Gleave (Warring ton Bull's Head) over E S Grange (Nlellor) whilst T Humphries (Rylands) beat T Radcliffe by 21-18. -- CRICKET NOTES. Warrington cannot be complimented on their display on Saturday, for they allowed Cheetharu to eispose of them in very cheap fashion. The result was the more surprising, as Cheetham were in poor form with the bat and were all out for the comparatively small total of 93. The visitors are usually a good batting side, but the Rev. H. Gray, who bowled throughout thel innings, gave them a very unpleasant time and bagged eight wickets for 41 runs. W. Bennetes bowling seems to lack the sting of his last season's deliveries and he is not accounting for as many wickets. Perhaps want of practice has something to do with it. Nobody expected Warringt in to be beaten, and it was rank bad batting that lost them the game. They made the mistake of over-carefulness and treated mediccre bowling with too much respect. H. Dolan made soine fine hits and was unlucky to be out at 10 to a brilliant catch in the slips. The condition of the ground fsvoured the bowlers somewhat, for it was very soft underneath and crusty on the surface. To-day Warrington entertain St. Helens Rees, hut, owing to the holiday-, the home team are not expected to place a very strong contingent in the field. -- There was a fine sporting finish in the match between Warrington 2nd and Cheetham 2nd. The Watringtonians had first turn at the wicket and made 119, W 1' Simpson distinguishing himself wi:b. a

The Warrington and District League competition yielded no surprises. Sankey and the Warrington Con- servatives are still on equal terms for the top position, each of them pulling off a victory on Saturday. Sankey entertained Rylands' Recreation, and an interesting game resulted in a triumph of the villagers by 12 runs. For Sankey the best batting display was that of T C Porter, who placed 29 to his credit. Newton batted finely for his 29 for Rylands. -- Warrington Conservatives were opposed by Cairo- street Unitarians, and in a low-scoring match, secured a majority of 14. C Holt was the most successful bats- man, his efforts realizing 17. D Plinston made a useful 12 for the Unitarians, and Dr Mellor put in some good bowling. -- St Helens Cable Company had to bow the knee to VVhitecross who amassed 55 and then dismissed their lopponents for 21. J Hutchinson batted admirably for the victors, his score being 20, while Barrow and Cald- well bowled effectively. The local breweries were pitted against each other, Wilderspool gaining the advantage by 9 runs. C G Stone knocked up an invaluable 19 and Rylance made 11 for Walker's. Orford v Cairo•street. -- In the Warrington and District Sunday School League, Orford St. John's created a surprise by defeat- ing Walton and compiling the highest score of the season-140. J Monks is batting well for Orford, having obtained the top score two weeks in succession. Mc Garr captured seven wickets for one run. -- A friendly match between St. Mary's and Monks Hall resulted in an easy win for the Saints by 30 runs. .1 McKay bowled excellently, taking eight wickets for 10 runs. Father Mawson wielded the willow in grand style and amassed 30. -- Latchford Baptists trounced Orford, thanks in a large measure to the bowling of Cliffs, who took six wickets for 22 runs. F Critchley contributed a capital not out innings of 17. Warrington Grammar School had to admit defeat at the hands of Oldham Grammar School who were 16 to the good. Hayes (20) was the top scorer for the Botelerians. -- Emmanuel Institute proved superior to Monks Hall, but only 11 runs separated the teams. There Was nothing particularly striking in the batting line, C Harrison giving the best display for 12. -- Bank-street Mission achieved a creditable victory at the expense of Holy Trinity, H Wright accounting for seven wickets for 15 runs. Winwick Asylum entertained Leigh Wesley Guild and were defeated by 29 runs. The highest scorers for the visitors were T Leather with 51 to his credit and .1 Hurst (39 not out). EMPLOYERS AND THE INSURANCE ACT.

THE EXAMJNER. SATURDAY JUNE 29, 1912.

The new assistant medical officer for Warrington—Dr. G. W. N. Joseph, M.D.—is a nephew of the Mayor and an "old boy" of the Grammar School. He had a brilliant career as a student and comes back to Warrington with many honours and a bright prospect. * * The price of household bread in Trance has risen to a figure equal to 9d for a 41b. loaf—a figure hardly reached since 1870, the year of the Franco-Prussian War. That is the result of Protection restricting importation at a time when the home supply is insufficient to meet the reeds of the people. Certainl y the taxes there do not make "everything cheaper."

I" The Cyclist's Note Book." Owing to pressure on our apace we have been com- pelled to hold over till next week "Silver Street's " cycling article. Farmhouse Fire. About 5.30 yesterday afternoon the Warrington Fire Brigade received a call to Peel Farm, Houghton Green, in the occupation of Mr. W. Stanier. The brigade, under Deputy-Superintendent Turner, were soon on the scene, and it was found that two large bales of pressed straw were on fire in a Dutch barn. The flames were put out after three hours hard work. The damage, which is estimated at ..4."200, is covered by insurance. Sankey Bridges Widening. Progress is being made with the work of widening Liverpool-road at Sankey Bridges, preparatory to an extensive scheme of improvement which will include the provision of a new steel bascule bridge to be operated by electric,ty. The portion of Liverpool. road within the Warrington Corporation boundary has already been widened to a uniformrtividth of 60ft. almost to Sankey Brook, and the Lancashire County Council 14 continuing the widening, which, however, will taper down to 451 t. to the canal embankment on its Warrington side. The" Times" engineering supplement adds that the contract for the road widening has been let to Mr H E Buckley, of Bradford, and it is expected that by the time the road work is completed the negotiations pending between the County Oouncil and the London and North-Western Railway Company for the replace- ment of the present swing budge over the canal by a bascule bridge will have been carried through. This bridge will be 401 t. wide between the parapets,

Pure Indian Tea ii the ideal of every household. India, the home of the tea plant, has a soil and climate admirably adapted to the production of the very highest class of ten. Then the British planter, working with British brains and British capital, has the most ingenious machinery the world can devise. In every process of manufacture there is the most scrupulous cleanliness, the leaf after being gathered being un- touched by hand. Indian Tea, therefore, is pure, rich, wholesome and economical. Among its varieties can be found some to suit every palate, every purse, and the water of every district. Ask your grocer for the Indian Tea that suits YOU.
